    Appendix A Selected Solutions

    I Foundations of Analysis
    1 Introductory Set Theory
    1.1 What Is a Set?
    1.1.4 Exercises

    Determine whether or not the given object is an element of the given set. Write your answer using the notation \(\in \) or \(\notin \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.1.4.1.

    Is \(3 \) an element of the set \(\set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \text{?}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    Yes, \(3 \in \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.2.

    Is \(5 \) an element of the set \(\set{ 3 , 9 , 12 } \text{?}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    No, \(3 \notin \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.3.

    Is \(8 \) an element of the set \(\set{ 0 , 2 , 4 , \dotsc , 12 } \text{?}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    If we interpret the roster notation as
    \begin{equation*} \set{ 0 , 2 , 4 , \dotsc , 12 } = \set{ 0 , 2 , 4 , 6 , 8 , 10 , 12 }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    then \(8 \in \set{ 0 , 2 , 4 , \dotsc , 12 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.4.

    Is \(50 \) an element of the set \(\set{ 0 , 1 , 4 , 9 , 16 , \dotsc } \text{?}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    If we interpret the roster notation as
    \begin{equation*} \set{ 0 , 1 , 4 , 9 , 16 , \dotsc } = \setmid{ n^{ 2 } }{ n \in \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , \dotsc } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    then \(50 \notin \set{ 0 , 1 , 4 , 9 , 16 , \dotsc } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Write down the specified set in roster notation.
    🔗

    1.1.4.5.

    Write down the set \(E \) of even numbers between \(1 \) and \(15 \) in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(E = \set{ 2 , 4 , 6 , 8 , 10 , 12 , 14 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.6.

    Write down the set \(L \) of letters in your full name in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The correct answer will differ depending on your full name. My full name is “Max Lahn”, and so I would answer
    \begin{equation*} L = \set{ \mathrm{ m } , \mathrm{ a } , \mathrm{ x } , \mathrm{ l } , \mathrm{ h } , \mathrm{ n } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.7.

    Write down the set \(C \) of colors in the rainbow in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The correct answer will differ depending on what you consider to be separate colors. I would answer
    \begin{equation*} C = \set{ \mathrm{ red } , \mathrm{ orange } , \mathrm{ yellow } , \mathrm{ green } , \mathrm{ blue } , \mathrm{ indigo } , \mathrm{ violet } } \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    🔗
    Let \(S \text{,}\) \(T \text{,}\) and \(U \) be the sets defined by
    \begin{align*} S \amp = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 } \amp T \amp = \set{ 3 , 4 } \amp U \amp = \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 }\text{.} \end{align*}
    Determine whether or not the stated set containment is true or false.
    🔗

    1.1.4.8.

    Is \(S \subseteq U \text{?}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    No, \(S \not \subseteq U \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.9.

    Is \(S \cap T \subsetneq U \text{?}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    Yes, \(S \cap T \subsetneq U \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Yes,
    \begin{equation*} S \cap T = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 } \cap \set{ 3 , 4 } = \emptyset \subsetneq \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } = U \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.10.

    Is \(U \subseteq S \cup T \text{?}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    Yes, \(U \subseteq S \cup T \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Yes,
    \begin{align*} U \amp = \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \subseteq \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 }\\ \amp = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 } \cup \set{ 3 , 4 } = S \cup T \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Write down the power sets of the given sets in roster notation.
    🔗

    1.1.4.11.

    Write down the power set \(\ps \of{ S } \) of the set \(S = \set{ 1 } \) in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The power set \(\ps \of{ S } \) of the set \(S = \set{ 1 } \) is the set
    \begin{equation*} \ps \of{ S } = \set{ \emptyset , \set{ 1 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Choosing a subset of the set \(S = \set{ 1 } \) is equivalent to making a unique binary choices of whether or not to include the element \(1 \text{.}\) We see that \(S \) has the following \(2 \) subsets: \(\emptyset \text{,}\) and \(\set{ 1 } \text{.}\) Therefore, the power set \(\ps \of{ S } \) of the set \(S = \set{ 1 } \) is the set
    \begin{equation*} \ps \of{ S } = \set{ \emptyset , \set{ 1 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.12.

    Write down the power set \(\ps \of{ T } \) of the set \(T = \set{ 1 , 2 } \) in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The power set \(\ps \of{ T } \) of the set \(T = \set{ 1 , 2 } \) is the set
    \begin{equation*} \ps \of{ T } = \set{ \emptyset , \set{ 1 } , \set{ 2 } , \set{ 1 , 2 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Choosing a subset of the set \(T = \set{ 1 , 2 } \) is equivalent to making a unique sequence of two binary choices of whether to include each element \(1 \) and \(2 \text{.}\) We see that \(T \) has the following \(4 \) subsets: \(\emptyset \text{,}\) \(\set{ 1 } \text{,}\) \(\set{ 2 } \text{,}\) and \(\set{ 1 , 2 } \text{.}\) Therefore, the power set \(\ps \of{ T } \) of the set \(S = \set{ 1 , 2 } \) is the set
    \begin{equation*} \ps \of{ T } = \set{ \emptyset , \set{ 1 } , \set{ 2 } , \set{ 1 , 2 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.13.

    Write down the power set \(\ps \of{ U } \) of the set \(U = \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \) in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The power set \(\ps \of{ U } \) of the set \(U = \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \) is the set
    \begin{equation*} \ps \of{ U } = \set{ \emptyset , \set{ 1 } , \set{ 2 } , \set{ 3 } , \set{ 1 , 2 } , \set{ 1 , 3 } , \set{ 2 , 3 } , \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Choosing a subset of the set \(U = \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \) is equivalent to making a unique sequence of three binary choices of whether to include each element \(1 \text{,}\) \(2 \text{,}\) and \(3 \text{.}\) We see that \(U \) has the following \(8 \) subsets: \(\emptyset \text{,}\) \(\set{ 1 } \text{,}\) \(\set{ 2 } \text{,}\) \(\set{ 3 } \text{,}\) \(\set{ 1 , 2 } \text{,}\) \(\set{ 1 , 3 } \text{,}\) \(\set{ 2 , 3 } \text{,}\) and \(\set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \text{.}\) Therefore, the power set \(\ps \of{ U } \) of the set \(U = \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \) is the set
    \begin{equation*} \ps \of{ U } = \set{ \emptyset , \set{ 1 } , \set{ 2 } , \set{ 3 } , \set{ 1 , 2 } , \set{ 1 , 3 } , \set{ 2 , 3 } , \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Compute the given set operations. Write your answer in roster or set-builder notation, or as a well-known set.
    🔗

    1.1.4.14.

    Compute \(S \cap T \text{,}\) where
    \begin{equation*} S = \setmid{ n \in \N }{ n = 2 k \textrm{ for some } k \in \N } = \set{ 0 , 2 , 4 , 6 , \dotsc } \end{equation*}
    is the set of even natural numbers and
    \begin{equation*} T = \setmid{ n \in \N }{ n = 2 k + 1 \textrm{ for some } k \in \N } = \set{ 1 , 3 , 5 , 7 , \dotsc } \end{equation*}
    is the set of odd integers.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(S \cap T = \emptyset \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    No natural number is both even and odd, and so \(S \cap T = \emptyset \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.15.

    Compute \(S \cup T \text{,}\) where \(S = \set{ 0 , 3 , 4 , 9 } \) and \(T = \set{ 1 , 3 , 6 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(S \cup T = \set{ 0 , 1 , 3 , 4 , 6 , 9 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    \(S \cup T \) contains all the elements of \(S \) and all the elements of \(T \text{,}\) so that \(S \cup T = \set{ 0 , 1 , 3 , 4 , 6 , 9 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.4.16.

    Compute \(S \setminus T \text{,}\) where
    \begin{equation*} S = \setmid{ n \in \N }{ n = 5 k \textrm{ for some } k \in \N } = \set{ 0 , 5 , 10 , 15 , \dotsc } \end{equation*}
    is the set of natural numbers divisible by \(5 \text{,}\) and
    \begin{equation*} T = \setmid{ n \in \N }{ n = 2 k \textrm{ for some } k \in \N } = \set{ 0 , 2 , 4 , 6 , \dotsc } \end{equation*}
    is the set of even natural numbers.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(S \setminus T = \setmid{ n \in \N }{ n = 10 k + 5 \textrm{ for some } k \in \N } = \set{ 5 , 15 , 25 , 35 , \dotsc } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    \(S \setminus T \) contains all the odd multiples of \(5 \text{,}\) so that
    \begin{align*} S \setminus T \amp = \setmid{ n \in \N }{ n = 10 k + 5 \textrm{ for some } k \in \N }\\ \amp = \set{ 5 , 15 , 25 , 35 , \dotsc }\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.5 Problems

    1.1.5.1. The distributive properties of set operations.

    1.1.5.1.a

    Prove that \(S \cap \of{ T \cup U } = \of{ S \cap T } \cup \of{ S \cap U } \) for all sets \(S \text{,}\) \(T \text{,}\) and \(U \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x \in S \cap \of{ T \cup U } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in S \) and \(x \in T \cup U \text{,}\) and so \(x \in T \) or \(x \in U \text{.}\) If \(x \in T \text{,}\) then \(x \in S \cap T \text{,}\) and so \(x \in \of{ S \cap T } \cup \of{ S \cap U } \text{.}\) On the other hand, if \(x \in U \text{,}\) then \(x \in S \cap U \text{,}\) and so \(x \in \of{ S \cap T } \cup \of{ S \cap U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in \of{ S \cap T } \cup \of{ S \cap U } \) for all elements \(x \in S \cap \of{ T \cup U } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} S \cap \of{ T \cup U } \subseteq \of{ S \cap T } \cup \of{ S \cap U }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Conversely, now let \(x \in \of{ S \cap T } \cup \of{ S \cap U } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in S \cap T \) or \(x \in S \cap U \text{.}\) If \(x \in S \cap T \text{,}\) then \(x \in S \) and \(x \in T \text{.}\) In particular, \(x \in T \cup U \text{,}\) and so \(x \in S \cap \of{ T \cup U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in S \cap \of{ T \cup U } \) for all \(x \in \of{ S \cap T } \cup \of{ S \cap U } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \of{ S \cap T } \cup \of{ S \cap U } \subseteq S \cap \of{ T \cup U }\text{;} \end{equation*}
    we now conclude that \(S \cap \of{ T \cup U } = \of{ S \cap T } \cup \of{ S \cap U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.5.1.b

    Prove that \(S \cup \of{ T \cap U } = \of{ S \cup T } \cap \of{ S \cup U } \) for all sets \(S \text{,}\) \(T \text{,}\) and \(U \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x \in S \cup \of{ T \cap U } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in S \) or \(x \in T \cap U \text{.}\) If \(x \in S \text{,}\) then \(x \in S \cup T \) and \(x \in S \cup U \text{,}\) and so \(x \in \of{ S \cup T } \cap \of{ S \cup U } \text{.}\) On the other hand, if \(x \in T \cap U \text{,}\) then \(x \in T \) and \(x \in U \text{.}\) In this case, \(x \in S \cup T \) and \(x \in S \cup U \text{,}\) and so \(x \in \of{ S \cup T } \cap \of{ S \cup U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in \of{ S \cup T } \cap \of{ S \cup U } \) for all elements \(x \in S \cup \of{ T \cap U } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} S \cup \of{ T \cap U } \subseteq \of{ S \cup T } \cap \of{ S \cup U }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Conversely, now let \(x \in \of{ S \cup T } \cap \of{ S \cup U } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in S \cup T \) and \(x \in S \cup U \text{.}\) In particular, if \(x \notin S \text{,}\) then \(x \in T \) and \(x \in U \text{,}\) and so \(x \in T \cap U \text{.}\) We conclude that \(x \in S \cup \of{ T \cap U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in S \cup \of{ T \cap U } \) for all \(x \in \of{ S \cup T } \cap \of{ S \cup U } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \of{ S \cup T } \cap \of{ S \cup U } \subseteq S \cup \of{ T \cap U }\text{;} \end{equation*}
    we now conclude that \(S \cup \of{ T \cap U } = \of{ S \cup T } \cap \of{ S \cup U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Taken together, these results are called the distributive properties of the set operations.
    🔗

    1.1.5.2. De Morgan’s laws.

    1.1.5.2.a

    Prove that \(S \setminus \of{ T \cap U } = \of{ S \setminus T } \cup \of{ S \setminus U } \) for all sets \(S \text{,}\) \(T \text{,}\) and \(U \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    First let \(x \in S \setminus \of{ T \cap U } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in S \) and \(x \notin T \cap U \text{.}\) We want to show that \(x \in S \setminus T \) or \(x \in S \setminus U \text{.}\) To that end, suppose that \(x \notin S \setminus T \text{.}\) Since \(x \in S \text{,}\) we must have \(x \in T \text{.}\)
    🔗
    But \(x \notin T \cap U \text{,}\) and so \(x \notin U \text{;}\) that is, \(x \in S \setminus U \text{.}\) Since if \(x \notin S \setminus T \text{,}\) then \(x \in S \setminus U \text{,}\) we conclude that \(x \in \of{ S \setminus T } \cup \of{ S \setminus U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Conversely, now let \(x \in \of{ S \setminus T } \cup \of{ S \setminus U } \text{,}\) so that \(x \in S \setminus T \) or \(x \in S \setminus U \text{.}\) If \(x \in S \setminus T \text{,}\) then \(x \in S \) and \(x \notin T \text{,}\) so that \(x \notin T \cap U \text{.}\) We conclude that \(x \in S \setminus \of{ T \cap U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in S \setminus \of{ T \cap U } \) if and only if \(x \in \of{ S \setminus T } \cup \of{ S \setminus U } \text{.}\) We conclude that \(S \setminus \of{ T \cap U } = \of{ S \setminus T } \cup \of{ S \setminus U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.5.2.b

    Prove that \(S \setminus \of{ T \cup U } = \of{ S \setminus T } \cap \of{ S \setminus U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    First let \(x \in S \setminus \of{ T \cup U } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in S \) and \(x \notin T \cup U \text{.}\) If \(x \in T \text{,}\) then \(x \in T \cup U \text{,}\) and so \(x \notin T \text{;}\) we conclude that \(x \in S \setminus T \text{.}\) Similarly, if \(x \in U \text{,}\) then \(x \in T \cup U \text{,}\) and so \(x \notin U \text{;}\) we conclude that \(x \in S \setminus U \text{.}\) Thus \(x \in \of{ S \setminus T } \cap \of{ S \setminus U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Conversely, now let \(x \in \of{ S \setminus T } \cap \of{ S \setminus U } \text{,}\) so that \(x \in S \setminus T \) and \(x \in S \setminus U \text{.}\) Thus \(x \in S \text{,}\) \(x \notin T \text{,}\) and \(x \notin U \text{.}\) If \(x \in T \cup U \text{,}\) then \(x \in T \) or \(x \in U \text{,}\) and so \(x \notin T \cup U \text{;}\) we conclude that \(x \in S \setminus \of{ T \cup U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in S \setminus \of{ T \cup U } \) if and only if \(x \in \of{ S \setminus T } \cap \of{ S \setminus U } \text{.}\) We conclude that \(S \setminus \of{ T \cup U } = \of{ S \setminus T } \cap \of{ S \setminus U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Taken together, these results are called De Morgan’s laws.
    🔗

    1.1.5.3. Power sets.

    Determine whether the following statement is true or false:
    The power set of a set contains at least two distinct elements.
    🔗
    🔗
    If the statement is true, prove it. If the statement is false, disprove it by providing a counterexample.
    🔗
    Solution.
    The statement is false. For a counterexample, we consider the empty set \(\emptyset \text{.}\) The only subset of the empty set \(\emptyset \) is \(\emptyset \) itself.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.5.4. Set operations and containment.

    Let \(S \) and \(T \) be sets.
    🔗

    1.1.5.4.a

    Prove that \(S \cap T = S \) if and only if \(S \subseteq T \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(S \cap T \subseteq S \text{,}\) it suffices to show that \(S \subseteq S \cap T \) if and only if \(S \subseteq T \text{.}\) To that end, note that if \(S \subseteq S \cap T \text{,}\) then (1) of Proposition 1.1.14 Set operations and containment implies that \(S \subseteq T \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Conversely, we note that \(S \subseteq S \text{,}\) so that if \(S \subseteq T \text{,}\) then (1) of Proposition 1.1.14 Set operations and containment implies that \(S \subseteq S \cap T \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.5.4.b

    Prove that \(S \cup T = S \) if and only if \(T \subseteq S \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(S \cup S \cup T \text{,}\) it suffices to show that \(S \cup T \subseteq S \) if and only if \(T \subseteq S \text{.}\) To that end, note that if \(S \cup T \subseteq S \text{,}\) then (2) of Proposition 1.1.14 Set operations and containment implies that \(T \subseteq S \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Conversely, we note that \(S \subseteq S \text{,}\) so that if \(T \subseteq S \text{,}\) then (2) of Proposition 1.1.14 Set operations and containment implies that \(S \cup T \subseteq S \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.1.5.4.c

    Prove that \(S \setminus T = S \) if and only if \(S \cap T = \emptyset \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    First suppose that \(S \cap T = \emptyset \text{.}\) Then \(x \notin T \) for all \(x \in S \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} S \setminus T = \setmid{ x }{ x \in S \textrm{ and } x \notin T } = \setmid{ x }{ x \in S } = S\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(S \cap T \neq \emptyset \text{.}\) Then \(x \in S \cap T \text{,}\) and so \(x \in S \) and \(x \in T \text{.}\) Since \(x \in T \text{,}\) \(x \notin S \setminus T \text{.}\) So \(x \) is an element of \(S \) but not \(S \setminus T \text{;}\) we conclude that \(S \setminus T \neq S \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2 Maps and Functions
    1.2.4 Exercises

    Write down the Cartesian products of the given sets in roster notation.
    🔗

    1.2.4.1.

    Write down the Cartesian product \(S \times T \) of the sets \(S = \set{ 0 , 1 } \) and \(T = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 } \) in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The Cartesian product \(S \times T \) of the sets \(S = \set{ 0 , 1 } \) and \(T = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 } \) is
    \begin{equation*} S \times T = \set{ \of{ 0 , 0 } , \of{ 0 , 1 } , \of{ 0 , 2 } , \of{ 1 , 0 } , \of{ 1 , 1 } , \of{ 1 , 2 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.2.

    Write down the Cartesian product \(U \times V \) of the sets \(U = \set{ 0 } \) and \(V = \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } \) in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The Cartesian product \(U \times V \) of the sets \(U = \set{ 0 } \) and \(V = \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } \) is
    \begin{equation*} U \times V = \set{ \of{ 0 , 1 } , \of{ 0 , 2 } , \of{ 0 , 3 } , \of{ 0 , 4 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.3.

    Write down the Cartesian product \(X \times Y \) of the sets \(X = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 } \) and \(Y = \emptyset \) in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The Cartesian product \(X \times Y \) of the sets \(X = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 } \) and \(Y = \emptyset \) is \(X \times Y = \emptyset \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Consider the sets \(X = \set{ 0 , 2 , 4 , 6 , 8 } \) and \(Y = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } \text{.}\) Determine whether or not the given object is a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.2.4.4.

    Determine whether or not
    \begin{equation*} f = \set{ \of{ 0 , 0 } , \of{ 2 , 1 } , \of{ 4 , 2 } , \of{ 6 , 3 } , \of{ 8 , 4 } } \end{equation*}
    is a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    Yes, \(f \colon X \to Y \) is a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.5.

    Determine whether or not
    \begin{equation*} g = \set{ \of{ 0 , 1 } , \of{ 2 , 1 } , \of{ 4 , 4 } , \of{ 8 , 1 } } \end{equation*}
    is a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    No, \(g \) is not a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    There is no output \(y \in Y \) so that \(\of{ 6 , y } \in g \text{,}\) and so \(g \) does not satisfy the totality condition. Thus \(g \) is not a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.6.

    Determine whether or not
    \begin{equation*} h = \set{ \of{ 0 , 1 } , \of{ 2 , 1 } , \of{ 4 , 4 } , \of{ 4 , 3 } , \of{ 6 , 2 } , \of{ 8 , 1 } } \end{equation*}
    is a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    No, \(h \) is not a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Note that \(\of{ 4 , 4 } , \of{ 4 , 3 } \in h \text{,}\) and so \(h \) does not satisfy the uniqueness condition. Thus \(h \) is not a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.7.

    Determine whether or not
    \begin{equation*} j = \set{ \of{ 0 , 0 } , \of{ 2 , 0 } , \of{ 4 , 0 } , \of{ 6 , 0 } , \of{ 8 , 0 } } \end{equation*}
    is a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    Yes, \(j \colon X \to Y \) is a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.8. Images and pre-images.

    Let \(f_{ 4 } \colon \set{ 1 , 2 } \to \set{ \mathrm{ red } , \mathrm{ blue } } \) be the map from \(\set{ 1 , 2 } \) to \(\set{ \mathrm{ red } , \mathrm{ blue } } \) defined in Example 1.2.4 Maps; that is,
    \begin{equation*} f_{ 4 } = \set{ \of{ 1 , \mathrm{ blue } } , \of{ 2 , \mathrm{ blue } } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Compute the given images and pre-images. Express your answer in roster notation if necessary.
    🔗

    1.2.4.8.a

    Compute the images \(f_{ 4 } \of{ 1 } \) and \(f_{ 4 } \of{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(f_{ 4 } \of{ 1 } = f_{ 4 } \of{ 2 } = \mathrm{ blue } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(\of{ 1 , \mathrm{ blue } } \in f_{ 4 } \text{,}\) the image \(f_{ 4 } \of{ 1 } \) of \(1 \) under \(f_{ 4 } \) is
    \begin{equation*} f_{ 4 } \of{ 1 } = \mathrm{ blue }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Similarly, since \(\of{ 2 , \mathrm{ blue } } \in f_{ 4 } \text{,}\) the image \(f_{ 4 } \of{ 2 } \) of \(2 \) under \(f_{ 4 } \) is
    \begin{equation*} f_{ 4 } \of{ 2 } = \mathrm{ blue }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.8.b

    Compute the image \(\im \of{ f_{ 4 } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\im \of{ f_{ 4 } } = \set{ \mathrm{ blue } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(f_{ 4 } \of{ x } = \mathrm{ blue } \) for all \(x \in \set{ 1 , 2 } \text{,}\) the image \(\im \of{ f_{ 4 } } \) of \(f_{ 4 } \) is
    \begin{equation*} \im \of{ f_{ 4 } } = f_{ 4 } \of{ \set{ 1 , 2 } } = \set{ \mathrm{ blue } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.8.c

    Compute the pre-images \(f_{ 4 }^{ -1 } \of{ \mathrm{ red } } \) and \(f_{ 4 }^{ -1 } \of{ \mathrm{ blue } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(f_{ 4 }^{ -1 } \of{ \mathrm{ red } } = \emptyset \) and \(f_{ 4 }^{ -1 } \of{ \mathrm{ blue } } = \set{ 1 , 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(f_{ 4 } \of{ x } = \mathrm{ blue } \) for all \(x \in \set{ 1 , 2 } \text{,}\) the pre-images \(f_{ 4 }^{ -1 } \of{ \mathrm{ red } } \) and \(f_{ 4 }^{ -1 } \of{ \mathrm{ blue } } \) of \(\mathrm{ red } \) and \(\mathrm{ blue } \) under \(f_{ 4 } \) are
    \begin{equation*} f_{ 4 }^{ -1 } \of{ \mathrm{ red } } = \setmid{ x \in \set{ 1 , 2 } }{ f_{ 4 } \of{ x } = \mathrm{ red } } = \emptyset \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{equation*} f_{ 4 }^{ -1 } \of{ \mathrm{ blue } } = \setmid{ x \in \set{ 1 , 2 } }{ f_{ 4 } \of{ x } = \mathrm{ blue } } = \set{ 1 , 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Determine whether or not the given map is injective, surjective, and/or bijective.
    🔗

    1.2.4.9.

    Let \(X = \set{ 0 , 1 } \) and \(Y = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 } \text{,}\) and consider the following map \(f \colon X \to Y \) from \(X \) to \(Y \text{:}\)
    \begin{equation*} f = \set{ \of{ 0 , 0 } , \of{ 1 , 0 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not \(f \) is injective, surjective, and/or bijective.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(f \) is neither injective, surjective, nor bijective.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.10.

    Let \(X = \set{ 0 , 1 } \) and \(Y = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 } \text{,}\) and consider the following map \(f \colon X \to Y \) from \(X \) to \(Y \text{:}\)
    \begin{equation*} f = \set{ \of{ 0 , 0 } , \of{ 1 , 2 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not \(f \) is injective, surjective, and/or bijective.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(f \) is injective, but it is neither surjective nor bijective.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.4.11.

    Let \(X = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 } \) and \(Y = \set{ 0 , 1 } \text{,}\) and consider the following map \(f \colon X \to Y \) from \(X \) to \(Y \text{:}\)
    \begin{equation*} f = \set{ \of{ 0 , 0 } , \of{ 1 , 1 } , \of{ 2 , 1 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not \(f \) is injective, surjective, and/or bijective.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(f \) is surjective, but it is neither injective nor bijective.

    1.2.5 Problems

    1.2.5.1. Non-commutativity of Cartesian products.

    Give examples of sets \(X \) and \(Y \) so that \(X \times Y \neq Y \times X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    If \(X = \set{ 0 } \) and \(Y = \set{ 1 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} X \times Y \amp = \set{ 0 } \times \set{ 1 } = \set{ \of{ 0 , 1 } }\\ \amp \neq \set{ \of{ 1 , 0 } } = \set{ 1 } \times \set{ 0 } = Y \times X\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.2. Existence of maps.

    Determine whether the following statement is true or false:
    For any sets \(X \) and \(Y \text{,}\) there is a map from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    If the statement is true, prove it. If the statement is false, disprove it by providing a counterexample.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(X = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 } \) and \(Y = \emptyset \text{.}\) As shown in Exercise Group 1.2.4.1–3 Cartesian products, \(X \times Y = \emptyset \text{.}\) In particular, no subset of \(X \times Y \) can satisfy the totality condition, and so there are no maps from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.3. Non-commutativity of composition.

    1.2.5.3.a

    Give examples of maps \(f \) and \(g \) so that the composition \(g \circ f \) is well-defined but the composition \(f \circ g \) is not.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(X = \set{ 0 } \text{,}\) \(Y = \set{ 1 } \text{,}\) and \(Z = \set{ 2 } \text{,}\) and consider the maps \(f \colon X \to Y \) and \(g \colon Y \to Z \) defined by \(f \of{ 0 } = 1 \) and \(g \of{ 1 } = 2 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Since the codomain \(Y \) of \(f \) is the domain of \(g \text{,}\) the composition \(g \circ f \) is well-defined. However, since the codomain \(Z \) of \(g \) is not the domain \(X \) of \(f \text{,}\) the composition \(f \circ g \) is not well-defined.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.3.b

    Give examples of maps \(f \) and \(g \) so that the compositions \(g \circ f \) and \(f \circ g \) are well-defined but \(g \circ f \neq f \circ g \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(X = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 } \text{,}\) and consider the maps \(f , g \colon X \to X \) from \(X \) to itself defined by
    \begin{align*} f \of{ 0 } \amp = 1 \amp f \of{ 1 } \amp = 2 \amp f \of{ 2 } \amp = 0\\ g \of{ 0 } \amp = 1 \amp g \of{ 1 } \amp = 0 \amp g \of{ 2 } \amp = 2 \end{align*}
    Then
    \begin{align*} \of{ g \circ f } \of{ 0 } \amp = g \of{ f \of{ 0 } } = g \of{ 1 } = 0\\ \amp \neq 2 = f \of{ 1 } = f \of{ g \of{ 0 } } = \of{ f \circ g } \of{ 0 }\text{,} \end{align*}
    and so \(g \circ f \neq f \circ g \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.4. Composition and injectivity/surjectivity/bijectivity.

    Let \(f \colon X \to Y \) and \(g \colon Y \to Z \) be maps between sets \(X \text{,}\) \(Y \text{,}\) and \(Z \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.2.5.4.a

    Prove that if \(f \) and \(g \) are injective, then so too is their composition \(g \circ f \colon X \to Z \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(\of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 1 } } = \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 2 } } \text{.}\) Then
    \begin{equation*} g \of{ f \of{ x_{ 1 } } } = \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 1 } } = \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 2 } } = g \of{ f \of{ x_{ 2 } } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so the injectivity of \(g \) implies that \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } = f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \text{.}\) Moreover, the injectivity of \(f \) implies that \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \text{.}\) In summary, we have shown that for all inputs \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) if \(\of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 1 } } = \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 2 } } \text{,}\) then \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \text{;}\) that is, \(g \circ f \) is injective.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.4.b

    Prove that if \(f \) and \(g \) are surjective, then so too is their composition \(g \circ f \colon X \to Z \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(z \in Z \text{.}\) Since \(g \) is surjective, \(z = g \of{ y } \) for some \(y \in Y \text{.}\) Moreover, since \(f \) is surjective, \(y = f \of{ x } \) for some \(x \in X \text{.}\) Thus
    \begin{equation*} z = g \of{ y } = g \of{ f \of{ x } } = \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    In summary, we have shown that for every output \(z \in Z \text{,}\) there is some input \(x \in X \) so that \(z = \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x } \text{;}\) that is, \(g \circ f \) is surjective.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.4.c

    Prove that if \(f \) and \(g \) are bijective, then so too is their composition \(g \circ f \colon X \to Z \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(f \) and \(g \) are bijective, they are both injective and surjective. (1) and (2) above not imply that their composition \(g \circ f \) is both injective and surjective; that is, \(g \circ f \) is bijective.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.4.d

    Prove that if the composition \(g \circ f \colon X \to Z \) is injective, then so too is \(f \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Suppose that \(f \) is not injective. Then \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } = f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \) for some distinct inputs \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{.}\) Note that
    \begin{equation*} \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 1 } } = g \of{ f \of{ x_{ 1 } } } = g \of{ f \of{ x_{ 2 } } } = \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 2 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Since \(\of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 1 } } = \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x_{ 2 } } \) but \(x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) \(g \circ f \) is not injective.
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that if \(f \) is not injective, then \(g \circ f \) is not injective. The contrapositive of this implication is the desired result: if \(g \circ f \) is injective, then so is \(f \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.4.e

    Prove that if the composition \(g \circ f \colon X \to Z \) is surjective, then so too is \(g \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Suppose that \(g \) is not surjective. Then there is some \(z \in Z \) so that \(z \neq g \of{ y } \) for all \(y \in Y \text{.}\) Note that
    \begin{equation*} \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x } = g \of{ f \of{ x } } \neq z \end{equation*}
    for all \(x \in X \text{,}\) and so \(g \circ f \) is not surjective.
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that if \(g \) is not surjective, then \(g \circ f \) is not surjective. The contrapositive of this implication is the desired result: if \(g \circ f \) is surjective, then so is \(g \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Note that the above results are used in the proof of Theorem 1.2.15 Invertibility. In order to avoid circular reasoning, you should prove the above statements by using only the definitions of injectivity, surjectivity, and bijectivity (and not the alternative characterizations given by Theorem 1.2.15 Invertibility and Corollary 1.2.16 Invertibility).
    🔗

    1.2.5.5. Indexed intersections and unions.

    Let \(\of{ U_{ j } }_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } } \) be a family of subsets \(U_{ j } \subseteq X \) of a set \(X \) indexed by a set \(\mathcal{ J } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.2.5.5.a

    Prove that for any subset \(V \subseteq X \text{,}\)
    \begin{equation*} V \subseteq \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \end{equation*}
    if and only if \(V \subseteq U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and
    \begin{equation*} \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \subseteq V \end{equation*}
    if and only if \(U_{ j } \subseteq V \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    First suppose that \(V \subseteq \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then for all \(x \in V \text{,}\)
    \begin{equation*} x \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \setmid{ x \in X }{ x \in U_{ j } \textrm{ for all } j \in \mathcal{ J } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(x \in U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) Thus \(V \subseteq U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(V \subseteq U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and let \(x \in V \text{.}\) Then \(x \in U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} x \in \setmid{ x \in X }{ x \in U_{ j } \textrm{ for all } j \in \mathcal{ J } } = \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    We conclude that \(V \subseteq \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Now suppose that \(\bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \subseteq V \text{.}\) Fix an index \(k \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and let \(x \in U_{ k } \text{.}\) Then
    \begin{equation*} x \in \setmid{ x \in X }{ x \in U_{ j } \textrm{ for some } j \in \mathcal{ J } } = \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(x \in V \text{.}\) We conclude that \(U_{ k } \subseteq V \text{,}\) and note that this holds for all indices \(k \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(U_{ j } \subseteq V \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and let \(x \in \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then
    \begin{equation*} x \in \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \setmid{ x \in X }{ x \in U_{ j } \textrm{ for some } j \in \mathcal{ J } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(x \in U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) Since \(U_{ j } \subseteq V \text{,}\) we have \(x \in V \text{.}\) We conclude that \(\bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \subseteq V \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.5.b

    Prove that
    \begin{equation*} \bigcap_{ k \in \mathcal{ K } }{ U_{ f \of{ k } } } = \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{equation*} \bigcup_{ k \in \mathcal{ K } }{ U_{ f \of{ k } } } = \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \end{equation*}
    for any set \(\mathcal{ K } \) and surjection \(f \colon \mathcal{ K } \to \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x \in X \text{,}\) and suppose first that \(x \in U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) Then \(f \of{ k } \in \mathcal{ J } \) for all indices \(k \in \mathcal{ K } \text{,}\) and so \(x \in U_{ f \of{ k } } \) for all such indices. Thus
    \begin{equation*} \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \subseteq \bigcap_{ k \in \mathcal{ K } }{ U_{ f \of{ k } } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Conversely, now suppose that \(x \in U_{ f \of{ k } } \) for all indices \(k \in \mathcal{ K } \text{.}\) Since \(f \colon \mathcal{ K } \to \mathcal{ J } \) is surjective, this implies that \(x \in U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) Thus
    \begin{equation*} \bigcap_{ k \in \mathcal{ K } }{ U_{ f \of{ k } } } \subseteq \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so
    \begin{equation*} \bigcap_{ k \in \mathcal{ K } }{ U_{ f \of{ k } } } = \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Now suppose that \(x \in U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) Since \(f \colon \mathcal{ K } \to \mathcal{ J } \) is surjective, \(f \of{ k } = j \) for some index \(k \in \mathcal{ K } \text{,}\) and so \(x \in U_{ j } = U_{ f \of{ k } } \text{.}\) In particular, \(x \in U_{ f \of{ k } } \) for some index \(k \in \mathcal{ K } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \subseteq \bigcup_{ k \in \mathcal{ K } }{ U_{ f \of{ k } } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Conversely, now suppose that \(x \in U_{ f \of{ k } } \) for some index \(k \in \mathcal{ K } \text{.}\) Since \(f \of{ k } \in \mathcal{ J } \) is surjective, this implies that \(x \in U_{ j } \) for some index \(j = f \of{ k } \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) Thus
    \begin{equation*} \bigcup_{ k \in \mathcal{ K } }{ U_{ f \of{ k } } } \subseteq \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so
    \begin{equation*} \bigcup_{ k \in \mathcal{ K } }{ U_{ f \of{ k } } } = \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.5.c

    Prove that
    \begin{equation*} V \cup \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ \of{ V \cup U_{ j } } } \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{equation*} V \cap \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ \of{ V \cap U_{ j } } } \end{equation*}
    for any subset \(V \subseteq X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(x \in V \cup \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in V \) or \(x \in U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) If \(x \in V \text{,}\) then \(x \in V \cup U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} x \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \cup U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    On the other hand, if \(x \notin V \text{,}\) then \(x \in U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so \(x \in V \cup U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} x \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \cup U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(x \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \cup U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in V \cup U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) If \(x \notin V \text{,}\) then \(x \in U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so \(x \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\) We conclude that
    \begin{equation*} x \in V \cup \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in V \cup \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \) if and only if \(x \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \cup U_{ j } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} V \cup \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \bigcap_{ k \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \cup U_{ k } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Now suppose that \(x \in V \cap \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in V \text{,}\) and \(x \in U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) In particular, \(x \in V \cap U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} x \in \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \cap U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(x \in \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \cap U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in V \cap U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so \(x \in V \) and \(x \in U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) We conclude that
    \begin{equation*} x \in V \cap \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in V \cap \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \) if and only if \(x \in \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \cap U_{ j } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} V \cap \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \cap U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.2.5.5.d

    Prove that
    \begin{equation*} V \setminus \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ \of{ V \setminus U_{ j } } } \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{equation*} V \setminus \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ \of{ V \setminus U_{ j } } } \end{equation*}
    for any subset \(V \subseteq X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(x \in V \setminus \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in V \) and \(x \notin \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\) So \(x \notin U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so \(x \in V \setminus U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} x \in \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \setminus U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(x \in \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \setminus U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in V \setminus U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so \(x \in V \) and \(x \notin U_{ j } \) for some index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) In particular, \(x \notin \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} x \in V \setminus \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in V \setminus \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \) if and only if \(x \in \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \setminus U_{ j } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} V \setminus \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \bigcup_{ k \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \setminus U_{ k } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Let \(x \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(x \in V \setminus \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in V \) and \(x \notin \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{.}\) So \(x \notin U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so \(x \in V \setminus U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} x \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \setminus U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(x \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \setminus U_{ j } } \text{.}\) Then \(x \in V \setminus U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so \(x \in V \) and \(x \notin U_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{.}\) In particular, \(x \notin \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} x \in V \setminus \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    In summary, we have shown that \(x \in V \setminus \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } \) if and only if \(x \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \setminus U_{ j } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} V \setminus \bigcup_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ U_{ j } } = \bigcap_{ k \in \mathcal{ J } }{ V \setminus U_{ k } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4 Finitary Operations and Relations
    1.4.4 Exercises

    1.4.4.1. Indexed Cartesian products.

    Let \(U_{ 1 } = \set{ 1 , 2 } \text{,}\) \(U_{ 2 } = \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \text{,}\) and \(U_{ 3 } = \set{ 0 , 1 } \text{,}\) and consider the Cartesian product \(U = \prod_{ k = 1 }^{ 3 }{ U_{ k } } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.4.4.1.a

    Write down the Cartesian product \(U = \prod_{ k = 1 }^{ 3 }{ U_{ k } } \) in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    We have
    \begin{align*} U \amp = \{ \of{ 1 , 1 , 0 } , \of{ 1 , 1 , 1 } , \of{ 1 , 2 , 0 } , \of{ 1 , 2 , 1 } , \of{ 1 , 3 , 0 } , \of{ 1 , 3 , 1 } ,\\ \amp \qquad \of{ 2 , 1 , 0 } , \of{ 2 , 1 , 1 } , \of{ 2 , 2 , 0 } , \of{ 2 , 2 , 1 } , \of{ 2 , 3 , 0 } , \of{ 2 , 3 , 1 } \}\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4.4.1.b

    For each index \(k \in \set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \text{,}\) let \(\pi_{ k } \colon U \to U_{ k } \) be the projection map onto the \(k \)th factor. Compute \(\pi_{ 1 } \of{ 2 , 3 , 0 } \text{,}\) \(\pi_{ 2 } \of{ 1 , 1 , 1 } \text{,}\) and \(\pi_{ 3 } \of{ 2 , 2 , 0 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    We have
    \begin{align*} \pi_{ 1 } \of{ 2 , 3 , 0 } \amp = 2 \amp \pi_{ 2 } \of{ 1 , 1 , 1 } \amp = 1 \amp \pi_{ 3 } \of{ 2 , 2 , 0 } \amp = 0\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4.4.2. Exponentiation of natural numbers.

    Let \(e \colon \N^{ 2 } \to \N \) be the exponentiation operation defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} e \of{ m , n } = m^{ n }\text{;} \end{equation*}
    that is, \(e \) is defined recursively by the formulae \(e \of{ m , 0 } = 1 \) and \(e \of{ m , n + 1 } = e^{ m , n } \cdot m \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.4.4.2.a

    Compute \(e \of{ 1 , 2 } \) and \(e \of{ 2 , 1 } \text{.}\) Is exponentiation a commutative binary operation?
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(e \of{ 1 , 2 } = 1^{ 2 } = 1 \) and \(e \of{ 2 , 1 } = 2^{ 1 } = 2 \text{.}\) Since \(e \of{ 1 , 2 } \neq e \of{ 2 , 1 } \text{,}\) exponentiation is non-commutative.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4.4.2.b

    Compute \(e \of{ e \of{ 2 , 1 } , 2 } \) and \(e \of{ 2 , e \of{ 1 , 2 } } \text{.}\) Is exponentiation an associative binary operation?
    🔗
    Answer.
    We compute
    \begin{equation*} e \of{ e \of{ 2 , 1 } , 2 } = e \of{ 2^{ 1 } , 2 } = e \of{ 2 , 2 } = 2^{ 2 } = 4 \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{equation*} e \of{ 2 , e \of{ 1 , 2 } } = e \of{ 2 , 1^{ 2 } } = e \of{ 2 , 1 } = 2^{ 1 } = 2\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Since \(e \of{ e \of{ 2 , 1 } , 2 } \neq e \of{ 2 , e \of{ 1 , 2 } } \text{,}\) exponentiation is non-associative.
    🔗
    🔗
    Let \(X = \set{ 0 , 1 , 2 , 3 , \dotsc , 10 } \text{.}\) Write down all the equivalence classes for the given equivalence relation on \(X \) in roster notation.
    🔗

    1.4.4.3.

    Consider the equivalence relation \(F \) on \(X \) defined by the condition that for all \(x , y \in X \text{,}\) \(x \mathrel{ F } y \) if and only if the English words for \(x \) and \(y \) start with the same letter. Write down the equivalence classes in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The equivalence classes are
    \begin{align*} \class{ 0 }_{ F } \amp = \set{ 0 }\\ \class{ 1 }_{ F } \amp = \set{ 1 }\\ \class{ 2 }_{ F } = \class{ 3 }_{ F } = \class{ 10 }_{ F } \amp = \set{ 2 , 3 , 10 }\\ \class{ 4 }_{ F } = \class{ 5 }_{ F } \amp = \set{ 4 , 5 }\\ \class{ 6 }_{ F } = \class{ 7 }_{ F } \amp = \set{ 6 , 7 }\\ \class{ 8 }_{ F } \amp = \set{ 8 }\\ \class{ 8 }_{ F } \amp = \set{ 8 }\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    \(0 \) is the only element of \(X \) beginning with Z, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 0 }_{ F } = \set{ 0 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(1 \) is the only element of \(X \) beginning with O, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 1 }_{ F } = \set{ 1 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(2 \text{,}\) \(3 \text{,}\) and \(10 \) are the only elements of \(X \) beginning with T, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 2 }_{ F } = \class{ 3 }_{ F } = \class{ 10 }_{ F } = \set{ 2 , 3 , 10 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(4 \) and \(5 \) are the only elements of \(X \) beginning with F, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 4 }_{ F } = \class{ 5 }_{ F } = \set{ 4 , 5 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(6 \) and \(7 \) are the only elements of \(X \) beginning with S, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 6 }_{ F } = \class{ 7 }_{ F } = \set{ 6 , 7 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(8 \) is the only element of \(X \) beginning with E, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 8 }_{ F } = \set{ 8 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Finally, \(9 \) is the only element of \(X \) beginning with N, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 9 }_{ F } = \set{ 9 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4.4.4.

    Consider the equivalence relation \(L \) on \(X \) defined by the condition that for all \(x , y \in X \text{,}\) \(x \mathrel{ L } y \) if and only if the English words for \(x \) and \(y \) end with the same letter. Write down all the equivalence classes in roster notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The equivalence classes are
    \begin{align*} \class{ 0 }_{ L } = \class{ 2 }_{ L } \amp = \set{ 0 , 2 }\\ \class{ 1 }_{ L } = \class{ 3 }_{ L } = \class{ 5 }_{ L } = \class{ 9 }_{ L } \amp = \set{ 1 , 3 , 5 , 9 }\\ \class{ 4 }_{ L } = \amp = \set{ 4 }\\ \class{ 6 }_{ L } = \amp = \set{ 6 }\\ \class{ 7 }_{ L } = \class{ 10 }_{ L } \amp = \set{ 7 , 10 }\\ \class{ 8 }_{ L } \amp = \set{ 8 }\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    \(0 \) and \(2 \) are the only elements of \(X \) ending with O, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 0 }_{ L } = \class{ 2 }_{ L } = \set{ 0 , 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(1 \text{,}\) \(3 \text{,}\) \(5 \text{,}\) and \(9 \) are the only elements of \(X \) ending with E, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 1 }_{ L } = \class{ 3 }_{ L } = \class{ 5 }_{ L } = \class{ 9 }_{ L } = \set{ 1 , 3 , 5 , 9 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(4 \) is the only element of \(X \) ending with R, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 4 }_{ L } = \set{ 4 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(6 \) is the only element of \(X \) ending with X, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 6 }_{ L } = \set{ 6 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(7 \) and \(10 \) are the only elements of \(X \) ending with N, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 7 }_{ L } = \class{ 10 }_{ L } = \set{ 7 , 10 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    \(8 \) is the only element of \(X \) ending with T, and so
    \begin{equation*} \class{ 8 }_{ L } = \set{ 8 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4.5 Problems

    1.4.5.1. Revisiting commutativity/associativity of Cartesian products.

    Recall from Problem 1.2.5.1 Non-commutativity of Cartesian products that there are sets \(X \) and \(Y \) so that \(X \times Y \neq Y \times X \text{.}\) Similarly, there are sets \(X \text{,}\) \(Y \text{,}\) and \(Z \) so that \(\of{ X \times Y } \times Z \neq X \times \of{ Y \times Z } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.4.5.1.a

    Prove that there is a bijection between \(X \times Y \) and \(Y \times X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Consider the maps \(f \colon X \times Y \to Y \times X \) and \(g \colon Y \times X \to X \times Y \) defined by the formulae \(f \of{ x , y } = \of{ y , x } \) and \(g \of{ y , x } = \of{ x , y } \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{equation*} \of{ g \circ f } \of{ x , y } = g \of{ f \of{ x , y } } = g \of{ y , x } = \of{ x , y } = \id_{ X \times Y } \of{ x , y } \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{equation*} \of{ f \circ g } \of{ y , x } = f \of{ g \of{ y , x } } = f \of{ x , y } = \of{ y , x } = \id_{ Y \times X } \of{ y , x } \end{equation*}
    for all elements \(x \in X \) and \(y \in Y \text{,}\) and so \(g \circ f = \id_{ X \times Y } \) and \(f \circ g = \id_{ Y \times X } \text{;}\) that is, \(f \) and \(g \) are inverses, and therefore bijections by Corollary 1.2.16 Invertibility.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4.5.1.b

    Prove that there is a bijection between \(\of{ X \times Y } \times Z \) and \(X \times \of{ Y \times Z } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Consider the maps \(f \colon \of{ X \times Y } \times Z \to X \times \of{ Y \times Z } \) and \(g \colon X \times \of{ Y \times Z } \to \of{ X \times Y } \times Z \) defined by the formulae \(f \of{ \of{ x , y } , z } = \of{ x , \of{ y , z } } \) and \(g \of{ x , \of{ y , z } } = \of{ \of{ x , y } , z } \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} \of{ g \circ f } \of{ \of{ x , y } , z } \amp = g \of{ f \of{ \of{ x , y } , z } } = g \of{ x , \of{ y , z } }\\ \amp = \of{ \of{ x , y } , z } = \id_{ \of{ X \times Y } \times Z } \of{ \of{ x , y } , z } \end{align*}
    and
    \begin{align*} \of{ f \circ g } \of{ x , \of{ y , z } } \amp = f \of{ g \of{ x , \of{ y , z } } } = f \of{ \of{ x , y } , z }\\ \amp = \of{ x , \of{ y , z } } = \id_{ X \times \of{ Y \times Z } } \of{ x , \of{ y , z } } \end{align*}
    for all elements \(x \in X \text{,}\) \(y \in Y \text{,}\) and \(z \in Z \text{,}\) and so \(g \circ f = \id_{ \of{ X \times Y } \times Z } \) and \(f \circ g = \id_{ X \times \of{ Y \times Z } } \text{;}\) that is, \(f \) and \(g \) are inverses, and therefore bijections by Corollary 1.2.16 Invertibility.
    🔗
    🔗
    While the sets \(X \times Y \) and \(Y \times X \) and the sets \(\of{ X \times Y } \times Z \) and \(X \times \of{ Y \times Z } \) are not equal, they may safely be identified via these bijections.
    🔗

    1.4.5.2. Cross products.

    The cross product \({ \times } \colon \of{ \R^{ 3 } }^{ 2 } \to \R^{ 3 } \) is the binary operation on \(3 \)-dimensional real coordinate space
    \begin{equation*} \R^{ 3 } = \setmid{ \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } }{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in \R } \end{equation*}
    defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \times \of{ y_{ 1 } , y_{ 2 } , y_{ 3 } } = \of{ x_{ 2 } y_{ 3 } - x_{ 3 } y_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } y_{ 1 } - x_{ 1 } y_{ 3 } , x_{ 1 } y_{ 2 } - x_{ 2 } y_{ 1 } } \end{equation*}
    For this problem, you may assume all standard properties of the arithmetic of real numbers, even though we will not formally define \(\R \) until Section 2.2 Constructing the Real Numbers.
    🔗

    1.4.5.2.a

    Determine whether or not the cross product \(\times \) on \(\R^{ 3 } \) is commutative. Justify your answer.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Consider the vectors \(\vect{ e }_{ 1 } = \of{ 1 , 0 , 0 } \text{,}\) \(\vect{ e }_{ 2 } = \of{ 0 , 1 , 0 } \text{,}\) and \(\vect{ e }_{ 3 } = \of{ 0 , 0 , 1 } \text{.}\) We note that
    \begin{align*} \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 2 } \amp = \of{ 1 , 0 , 0 } \times \of{ 0 , 1 , 0 }\\ \amp = \of{ 0 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 1 , 0 \cdot 0 - 1 \cdot 0 , 1 \cdot 1 - 0 \cdot 0 } = \of{ 0 , 0 , 1 } = \vect{ e }_{ 3 } \end{align*}
    and
    \begin{align*} \vect{ e }_{ 2 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \amp = \of{ 0 , 1 , 0 } \times \of{ 1 , 0 , 0 }\\ \amp = \of{ 1 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 0 , 0 \cdot 1 - 0 \cdot 0 , 0 \cdot 0 - 1 \cdot 1 } = \of{ 0 , 0 , -1 } = -\vect{ e }_{ 3 }\text{.} \end{align*}
    Since
    \begin{equation*} \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 2 } = \vect{ e }_{ 3 } \neq -\vect{ e }_{ 3 } = \vect{ e }_{ 2 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 1 }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    the cross product \(\times \) is non-commutative.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4.5.2.b

    Determine whether or not the cross product \(\times \) on \(\R^{ 3 } \) is associative. Justify your answer.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Consider the vectors \(\vect{ e }_{ 1 } = \of{ 1 , 0 , 0 } \text{,}\) \(\vect{ e }_{ 2 } = \of{ 0 , 1 , 0 } \text{,}\) and \(\vect{ e }_{ 3 } = \of{ 0 , 0 , 1 } \text{.}\) We note that
    \begin{align*} \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \amp = \of{ 1 , 0 , 0 } \times \of{ 1 , 0 , 0 }\\ \amp = \of{ 0 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 0 , 0 \cdot 1 - 1 \cdot 0 , 1 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 1 } = \of{ 0 , 0 , 0 } = \vect{ 0 }\text{,} \end{align*}
    \begin{align*} \vect{ 0 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 2 } \amp = \of{ 0 , 0 , 0 } \times \of{ 0 , 1 , 0 }\\ \amp = \of{ 0 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 1 , 0 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 0 , 0 \cdot 1 - 0 \cdot 0 } = \of{ 0 , 0 , 0 } = \vect{ 0 }\text{,} \end{align*}
    and
    \begin{align*} \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 3 } \amp = \of{ 1 , 0 , 0 } \times \of{ 0 , 0 , 1 }\\ \amp = \of{ 0 \cdot 1 - 0 \cdot 0 , 0 \cdot 0 - 1 \cdot 1 , 1 \cdot 0 - 0 \cdot 0 } = \of{ 0 , -1 , 0 } = -\vect{ e }_{ 2 }\text{.} \end{align*}
    Together with the computations above in (a), we see that
    \begin{align*} \of{ \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 1 } } \times \vect{ e }_{ 2 } \amp = \vect{ 0 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 2 } = \vect{ 0 }\\ \amp = -\vect{ e }_{ 2 } = \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 3 } = \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \times \of{ \vect{ e }_{ 1 } \times \vect{ e }_{ 2 } }\text{,} \end{align*}
    and so the cross product \(\times \) is non-associative.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4.5.3. Generating equivalence relations.

    Let \(X \) be a set.
    🔗

    1.4.5.3.a

    Prove that the intersection \(R = \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ R_{ j } } \) of any indexed family \(\of{ R_{ j } }_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } } \) of equivalence relations \(R_{ j } \) on \(X \) is an equivalence relation on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x \in X \text{.}\) Since for each index \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) the equivalence relation \(R_{ j } \) is reflexive, we have \(\of{ x , x } \in R_{ j } \text{.}\) Thus
    \begin{equation*} \of{ x , x } \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ R_{ j } } = R\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(R \) is reflexive.
    🔗
    Now let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(\of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \in R \text{.}\) Then \(\of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \in R_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so \(\of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } \in R_{ j } \) for all such indices by symmetry. Thus
    \begin{equation*} \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ R_{ j } } = R\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(R \) is symmetric.
    🔗
    Finally, let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(\of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } , \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \in R \text{.}\) Then \(\of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } , \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \in R_{ j } \) for all indices \(j \in \mathcal{ J } \text{,}\) and so \(\of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \in R_{ j } \) for all such indices by transitivity. Thus
    \begin{equation*} \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \in \bigcap_{ j \in \mathcal{ J } }{ R_{ j } } = R\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(R \) is transitive. Since \(R \) is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive, it is an equivalence relation on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.4.5.3.b

    Prove that for any binary relation \(R \) on \(X \text{,}\) there is a unique equivalence relation \(S \) on \(X \) which satisfies the following conditions:
    1. \(S \) is a refinement of \(R \text{;}\) that is, \(R \subseteq S \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    2. \(S \) is coarser than any equivalence relation which refines \(R \text{;}\) that is, \(S \subseteq T \) for any equivalence relation \(T \) on \(X \) so that \(R \subseteq T \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Denote by \(\mathcal{ T } \) the set of equivalence relations on \(X \) which contain \(R \text{,}\) and let
    \begin{equation*} S = \bigcap_{ T \in \mathcal{ T } }{ T }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    By (a) above, \(S \) is an equivalence relation on \(X \text{.}\) Since \(R \subseteq T \) for all \(T \in \mathcal{ T } \text{,}\) (a) of Problem 1.2.5.5 Indexed intersections and unions implies that \(R \subseteq S \text{;}\) that is, \(S \) satisfies condition (1). On the other hand, \(S \subseteq T \) for all \(T \in \mathcal{ T } \text{,}\) and so \(S \) satisfies condition (2).
    🔗
    Uniqueness of \(S \) follows from the fact that \(S \in \mathcal{ T } \text{;}\) specifically, if an equivalence \(T \) satisfies condition (1), then \(T \in \mathcal{ T } \) and so \(S \subseteq T \text{.}\) If \(T \) also satisfies condition (2), then \(T \subseteq S \text{,}\) and so \(T = S \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    The unique equivalence relation on \(X \) constructed in (b) above is called the equivalence relation generated by \(R \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.4.5.4. Universal property of quotients.

    Let \(\equiv \) be an equivalence relation on a set \(X \text{.}\) A map \(f \colon X \to Y \) from \(X \) to a set \(Y \) is called \(\equiv \)-invariant if for all elements \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) if \(x_{ 1 } \equiv x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } = f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Prove that for every \(\equiv \)-invariant map \(f \colon X \to Y \text{,}\) there is a map \(\overline{ f } \colon X / { \equiv } \to Y \) so that \(\overline{ f } \circ \pi_{ \equiv } = f \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(\class{ x_{ 1 } }_{ \equiv } = \class{ x_{ 2 } }_{ \equiv } \text{.}\) Then \(x_{ 1 } \equiv x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) and so \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } = f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \text{.}\) We conclude that the map \(\overline{ f } \colon X / { \equiv } \to Y \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} \overline{ f } \of{ \class{ x }_{ \equiv } } = f \of{ x } \end{equation*}
    is well-defined. Moreover, we note that
    \begin{equation*} \of{ \overline{ f } \circ \pi_{ \equiv } } \of{ x } = \overline{ f } \of{ \pi_{ \equiv } \of{ x } } = \overline{ f } \of{ \class{ x }_{ \equiv } } = f \of{ x } \end{equation*}
    for all inputs \(x \in X \text{,}\) and so \(\overline{ f } \circ \pi_{ \equiv } = f \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5 Introduction to Order Theory
    1.5.4 Exercises

    Determine whether or not the given preorder is a partial order. If the preorder is a partial order, determine whether or not it is total.
    🔗

    1.5.4.1.

    Let \(R \) be the preorder on the set \(\set{ 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } \) defined by
    \begin{equation*} \set{ \of{ 4 , 2 } , \of{ 4 , 3 } , \of{ 2 , 1 } , \of{ 3 , 1 } , \of{ 4 , 1 } , \of{ 1 , 3 } , \of{ 2 , 3 } , \of{ 4 , 4 } , \of{ 3 , 3 } , \of{ 2 , 2 } , \of{ 1 , 1 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not \(R \) is a partial order on \(\set{ 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } \text{.}\) If so, determine whether or not it is total.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(R \) is not a partial order.
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We observe that \(\of{ 1 , 3 } , \of{ 3 , 1 } \in R \text{,}\) but \(1 \neq 3 \text{.}\) Thus \(R \) is not anti-symmetric, and so \(R \) is not a partial order.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.4.2.

    Let \(\precsim \) be the preorder on the set \(P \) of all people (living or dead) defined so that for all people \(p , q \in P \text{,}\) \(p \precsim q \) if and only if either \(p \) is either \(q \) or an ancestor of \(q \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(\precsim \) is a partial order on \(P \text{.}\) If so, determine whether or not it is total.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\precsim \) is a partial order but not a total order.
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(p , q \in P \) be people, and suppose that \(p \preceq q \) and \(q \preceq p \text{.}\) If \(p \) and \(q \) are different people, then it is not possible for each to be the ancestor of the other (since ancestors are born strictly before their descendants). Thus \(p \) and \(q \) are the same person. We conclude that the preorder \(\precsim \) is anti-symmetric and therefore a partial order.
    🔗
    However, \(\precsim \) is not a total order, since for example my siblings and I are not comparable. For example, my sister is not my ancestor, and I am not my sister’s ancestor.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.4.3.

    Let \(\precsim \) be the preorder on the set \(C \) of all circles in the Cartesian plane so that for all circles \(c , d \in C \text{,}\) \(c \precsim d \) if and only if the diameter of \(c \) is at most the diameter of \(d \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(\precsim \) is a partial order on \(C \text{.}\) If so, determine whether or not it is total.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\precsim \) is not a partial order.
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since there are distinct circles with the same diameter, \(\precsim \) is not anti-symmetric, and therefore is not a partial order.
    🔗
    🔗
    Determine whether or not the following subsets of the partially ordered set \(\ps \of{ \N } \) are bounds for the given element.
    🔗

    1.5.4.4.

    Which of the following are upper bounds for the set \(\set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \) in the partially ordered set \(\ps \of{ \N } \text{?}\)
    🔗
    • \(\emptyset \)
    • \(\set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 0 } \)
    • \(\N \)

    1.5.4.5.

    Which of the following are lower bounds for the set \(\set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \) in the partially ordered set \(\ps \of{ \N } \text{?}\)
    🔗
    • \(\emptyset \)
    • \(\set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\set{ 1 , 2 , 3 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 0 } \)
    • \(\N \)
    Determine the boundedness of the following subsets of the totally ordered set \(\N \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.5.4.6.

    Which of the following subsets of the totally ordered set \(\N \) are bounded from below?
    🔗
    • \(\emptyset \)
    • \(\set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 0 } \)
    • \(\N \)

    1.5.4.7.

    Which of the following subsets of the totally ordered set \(\N \) are bounded from above?
    🔗
    • \(\emptyset \)
    • \(\set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 0 } \)
    • \(\N \)

    1.5.4.8.

    Which of the following subsets of the totally ordered set \(\N \) are bounded?
    🔗
    • \(\emptyset \)
    • \(\set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 1 , 2 } \)
    • \(\N \setminus \set{ 0 } \)
    • \(\N \)

    1.5.5 Problems

    1.5.5.1. Order by divisibility.

    Consider the binary relation \(\divides \) on the natural numbers \(\N \) defined so that for all natural numbers \(m , n \in \N \text{,}\) \(m \divides n \) if and only if \(m k = n \) for some natural number \(k \in \N \text{;}\) that is, \(m \divides n \) if and only if \(n \) is a multiple of \(m \text{.}\)
    🔗

    1.5.5.1.a

    Prove that \(\divides \) is a partial order on the set \(\N \) of natural numbers.
    🔗
    Solution.
    We must prove that the binary relation \(\divides \) is reflexive, anti-symmetric, and transitive. To that end, first let \(n \in \N \) be a natural number, and note that \(n \cdot 1 = n \text{.}\) Thus \(n \divides n \text{,}\) and so the binary relation \(\divides \) is reflexive.
    🔗
    Now let \(m , n \in \N \) be natural numbers, and suppose that \(m \divides n \) and \(n \divides m \text{.}\) Then \(m k = n \) and \(n l = m \) for some natural numbers \(k , l \in \N \text{.}\) If \(m = 0 \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} n = m k = 0 k = 0 = m\text{.} \end{equation*}
    On the other hand, if \(m \neq 0 \text{,}\) then we observe that
    \begin{equation*} m \cdot 1 = m = n l = \of{ m k } l = m \of{ k l } ,\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so Corollary 1.3.16 Cancellation of multiplication implies that \(k l = 1 \text{.}\) Since \(k , l \in \N \) are natural numbers, we must have \(k = l = 1 \text{,}\) so that
    \begin{equation*} m = n l = n \cdot 1 = n\text{.} \end{equation*}
    In summary, we have shown that if \(m \divides n \) and \(n \divides m \text{,}\) then \(m = n \text{.}\) Thus the binary relation \(\divides \) is anti-symmetric
    🔗
    Finally, let \(m , n , p \in \N \) be natural numbers, and suppose that \(m \divides n \) and \(n \divides p \text{.}\) Then \(m k = n \) and \(n l = p \) for some natural numbers \(k , l \in \N \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} m \of{ k l } = \of{ m k } l = n l = p\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Thus the binary relation \(\divides \) is transitive.
    🔗
    Since the binary relation \(\divides \) is reflexive, anti-symmetric, and transitive, it is a partial order on \(\N \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.1.b

    Is the binary relation \(\divides \) a total order on \(\N \text{?}\) Justify your answer.
    🔗
    Solution.
    No, the binary relation \(\divides \) is not a total order on \(\N \text{,}\) since for example \(2 \not \divides 3 \) and \(3 \not \divides 2 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.1.c

    What are the extrema of the partially ordered set \(\of{ \N , { \divides } } \text{?}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    For all natural numbers \(n \in \N \text{,}\) \(1 n = n \) and \(0 n = 0 \text{,}\) so that \(1 \divides n \divides 0 \text{.}\) Thus \(1 \) and \(0 \) are the minimum and maximum of the partially ordered set \(\of{ \N , { \divides } } \text{,}\) respectively.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.1.d

    We will abuse notation and denote the restriction of \(\divides \) to the set \(\N \setminus \set{ 0 , 1 } \) by \(\divides \) as well. What are the extremal elements of the partially ordered set \(\of{ \N \setminus \set{ 0 , 1 } , { \divides } } \text{?}\) Are these extremal elements extrema?
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(n \in \N \setminus \set{ 0 , 1 } \text{,}\) and note that \(n \divides 2 n \) but \(n \neq 2 n \text{.}\) Thus there are no maximal elements of the partially ordered set \(\of{ \N \setminus \set{ 0 , 1 } , { \divides } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Conversely, let \(p \in \N \) be a prime number. Since the only natural number divisors of \(p \) are \(1 \) and \(p \text{,}\) \(p \) is a minimal element of the partially ordered set \(\of{ \N \setminus \set{ 0 , 1 } , { \divides } } \text{.}\) On the other hand, any composite natural number has proper divisors, and so is not minimal.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.2. Extremal subsets.

    Find the extremal elements of the partially ordered set \(\ps \of{ \N } \setminus \set{ \emptyset , \N } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Denote by \(X \) the partially ordered set \(\ps \of{ \N } \setminus \set{ \emptyset , \N } \text{.}\) For all natural numbers \(n \in \N \text{,}\) the only subsets of the set \(\set{ n } \) are \(\emptyset \) and \(\set{ n } \) itself. Thus \(\set{ n } \) is minimal in \(X \text{.}\) On the other hand, any proper subset of \(\N \) which contains at least two natural numbers has a proper non-empty subset, and so is not minimal in \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    For all natural numbers \(n \in \N \text{,}\) the only subsets of \(\N \) which contain \(\N \setminus \set{ n } \) are \(\N \) and \(\N \setminus \set{ n } \) itself. Thus \(\N \setminus \set{ n } \) is maximal in \(X \text{.}\) On the other hand, any non-empty subset of \(\N \) which misses at least two natural numbers is contained in a strictly larger proper subset, and so is not maximal in \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.3. Opposite of an order.

    The opposite \(\op{ R } \) of a binary relation \(R \) on a set \(X \) is the binary relation defined by
    \begin{equation*} \op{ R } = \setmid{ \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } \in X^{ 2 } }{ \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \in R }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗

    1.5.5.3.a

    Prove that the opposite of a preorder is a preorder.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\precsim \) be a preorder on a set \(X \text{,}\) and denote by \(\succsim \) the opposite. We will show that the binary operation \(\succsim \) is reflexive and transitive. To that end, first let \(x \in X \text{.}\) The reflexivity of \(\precsim \) implies that \(x \precsim x \text{,}\) and so \(x \succsim x \text{.}\) Thus the binary relation \(\succsim \) is reflexive.
    🔗
    Now let \(x , y , z \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(x \succsim y \) and \(y \succsim z \text{.}\) Then \(z \precsim y \) and \(y \precsim x \text{,}\) and so the transitivity of \(\precsim \) implies that \(z \precsim x \text{.}\) Thus \(x \succsim z \text{,}\) and so the binary relation \(\succsim \) is transitive.
    🔗
    Since the binary relation \(\succsim \) is reflexive and transitive, it is a preorder on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.3.b

    Prove that the opposite of a partial order is a partial order.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\preceq \) be a partial order on a set \(X \text{,}\) and denote by \(\succeq \) the opposite. Since \(\preceq \) is a preorder, \(\succeq \) is a preorder on \(X \) by (a) above. Thus \(\succeq \) is reflexive and transitive, and so it remains to show that \(\succeq \) is anti-symmetric.
    🔗
    To that end, let \(x , y \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that both \(x \succeq y \) and \(y \succeq x \text{.}\) Then \(y \preceq x \) and \(x \preceq y \text{,}\) and so the anti-symmetry of \(\preceq \) implies that \(x = y \text{.}\) Thus \(\succeq \) is anti-symmetric.
    🔗
    Since the binary relation \(\succeq \) is reflexive, anti-symmetric, and transitive, it is a partial order on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.3.c

    Prove that the opposite of a total order is a total order.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\leq \) be a total order on a set \(X \text{,}\) and denote by \(\geq \) the opposite. Since \(\leq \) is a partial order, \(\geq \) is a partial order on \(X \) by (b) above. Thus \(\geq \) is reflexive, anti-symmetric, and transitive, and so it remains to show that \(\geq \) is total. To that end, let \(x , y \in X \text{.}\) The totality of \(\leq \) implies that either \(x \leq y \) or \(y \leq x \text{.}\) Thus \(y \geq x \) and \(x \geq y \text{,}\) and so the binary relation \(\geq \) is total.
    🔗
    Since \(\geq \) is anti-symmetric, transitive, and total, it is a total order on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.4. Orders from pullbacks.

    1.5.5.4.a

    Prove that the pullback of a preorder is a preorder.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Consider the pullback \(\precsim_{ f } \) of a preorder \(\precsim \) on a set \(Y \) along a map \(f \) from a set \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\) For all elements \(x \in X \text{,}\) the reflexivity of \(\precsim \) implies that \(f \of{ x } \precsim f \of{ x } \text{,}\) and so \(x \precsim_{ f } x \text{;}\) that is, \(\precsim_{ f } \) is reflexive.
    🔗
    For all elements \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \text{,}\) suppose that \(x_{ 1 } \precsim_{ f } x_{ 2 } \) and \(x_{ 2 } \precsim_{ f } x_{ 3 } \text{.}\) Then \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } \precsim f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \) and \(f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \precsim f \of{ x_{ 3 } } \text{,}\) and so the transitivity of \(\precsim \) implies that \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } \precsim f \of{ x_{ 3 } } \text{;}\) that is, \(x_{ 1 } \precsim_{ f } x_{ 3 } \text{,}\) and so \(\precsim_{ f } \) is transitive.
    🔗
    Since the binary relation \(\precsim_{ f } \) is reflexive and transitive, it is a preorder on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.4.b

    Prove that the pullback \(\preceq_{ f } \) of a partial order \(\preceq \) on a set \(Y \) along a map \(f \colon X \to Y \) is a partial order on \(X \) if and only if \(f \) is injective.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(\preceq \) is a partial order on \(Y \text{,}\) it is a preorder on \(Y \text{,}\) and thus \(\preceq_{ f } \) is a preorder on \(X \) by (a) above. Thus it suffices to show that \(\preceq_{ f } \) is anti-symmetric if and only if \(f \) is injective. To that end, suppose first that \(\preceq_{ f } \) is not anti-symmetric. Then \(x_{ 1 } \preceq_{ f } x_{ 2 } \) and \(x_{ 2 } \preceq_{ f } x_{ 1 } \) for some elements \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \) so that \(x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 2 } \text{.}\) We observe that \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } \preceq f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \) and \(f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \preceq f \of{ x_{ 1 } } \text{,}\) and so the anti-symmetry of \(\preceq \) implies that \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } = f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \text{.}\) Since \(x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 2 } \) but \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } = f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \text{,}\) \(f \) is not injective.
    🔗
    Conversely, suppose that \(f \) is not injective. Then \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } = f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \) for some elements \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \) so that \(x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 2 } \text{.}\) We see that \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } \preceq f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \) and \(f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \preceq f \of{ x_{ 1 } } \text{,}\) so that \(x_{ 1 } \preceq_{ f } x_{ 2 } \) and \(x_{ 2 } \preceq_{ f } x_{ 1 } \text{.}\) That \(x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 2 } \) now implies that \(\preceq_{ f } \) is not anti-symmetric.
    🔗
    🔗

    1.5.5.4.c

    Is the pullback of a total order along an injective map necessarily a total order? If so, prove it. If not, provide a counterexample.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Yes, the pullback of a total order along an injective map is necessarily a total order. Indeed, consider the pullback \(\leq_{ f } \) of a total order \(\leq \) on a set \(Y \) along an injective map \(f \colon X \to Y \) from a set \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\) Since \(\leq \) is a total order, Lemma 1.5.14 Total orders are partial orders implies that it is a partial order, and so \(\leq_{ f } \) is a partial order by (b) above. Thus it suffices to show that \(\leq_{ f } \) must be total.
    🔗
    To that end, let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) and note that the totality of \(\leq \) implies that either \(f \of{ x_{ 1 } } \leq f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \) or \(f \of{ x_{ 2 } } \leq f \of{ x_{ 1 } } \) (or both); that is, either \(x_{ 1 } \leq_{ f } x_{ 2 } \) or \(x_{ 2 } \leq_{ f } x_{ 1 } \) (or both). Thus \(\leq_{ f } \) is total, and hence a total order on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    2 Constructing the Number Systems
    2.2 Constructing the Real Numbers
    2.2.6 Problems

    2.2.6.1. Cancellation of addition.

    Prove that for all Dedekind cuts \(X , Y , Z \in \R \text{,}\) if \(X + Z \subseteq Y + Z \text{,}\) then \(X \subseteq Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    If \(X + Z \subseteq Y + Z \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} X = X + X_{ 0 } = X + Z + \of{ -Z } \leq Y + Z + \of{ -Z } = Y + X_{ 0 } = Y \end{equation*}
    by Theorem 2.2.15 Arithmetic of Dedekind cuts.
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3 The Complete Ordered Field
    2.3.3 Exercises

    Determine which of the ordered field axioms are satisfied by the given algebraic structure.
    🔗

    2.3.3.1.

    Which of the ordered field axioms are not satisfied by the set \(\Z \) of integers?
    🔗
    Solution.
    The integers \(\Z \) satisfy all the ordered field axioms except the existence of multiplicative inverses. In fact, only \(1 \) and \(-1 \) have multiplicative inverses.
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.3.2.

    Which of the ordered field axioms are not satisfied by the set \(\N \) of natural numbers?
    🔗
    Solution.
    The natural numbers \(\N \) satisfy all the ordered field axioms except the existence of additive and multiplicative inverses. In fact, only \(0 \) has an additive inverse, and only \(1 \) has a multiplicative inverse.
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.3.3.

    Let \(X \) be a set. Which of the ordered field axioms are not satisfied by the ordered quadruple \(\of{ \ps \of{ X } , \cup , \cap , \subseteq } \text{?}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    The ordered quadruple \(\of{ \ps \of{ X } , \cap , \cup , \subseteq } \) satisfies all the ordered field axioms except the existence additive inverses, the existence of multiplicative inverses, and totality.
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4 Problems

    2.3.4.1. Uniqueness in a field.

    Let \(\of{ F , + , \cdot } \) be a field
    🔗

    2.3.4.1.a

    Show that the additive identity element \(0 \in F \) is unique.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x \in F \text{,}\) and suppose that \(x \) is an additive identity element; that is, suppose that \(x + y = y + x = y \) for all \(y \in F \text{.}\) Then in particular,
    \begin{equation*} x = x + 0 = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.1.b

    Show that the multiplicative identity element \(1 \in F \) is unique.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x \in F \text{,}\) and suppose that \(x \) is a multiplicative identity element; that is, suppose that \(x \cdot y = y \cdot x = y \) for all \(y \in F \text{.}\) Then in particular,
    \begin{equation*} x = x \cdot 1 = 1\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.1.c

    Show that additive inverses are unique; that is, given elements \(x , y , z \in F \text{,}\) show that if
    \begin{equation*} x + y = x + z = 0\text{,} \end{equation*}
    then \(y = z \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Suppose that \(x + y = x + z = 0 \) for some elements \(x , y , z \in F \text{,}\) and note that
    \begin{align*} y \amp = y + 0 = y + x + z\\ \amp = x + y + z = 0 + z = z\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.1.d

    Show that multiplicative inverses are unique; that is, given elements \(x , y , z \in F \text{,}\) show that if
    \begin{equation*} x \cdot y = x \cdot z = 1\text{,} \end{equation*}
    then \(y = z \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Suppose that \(x \cdot y = x \cdot z = 1 \) for some elements \(x , y , z \in F \text{,}\) and note that
    \begin{align*} y \amp = y \cdot 1 = y \cdot x \cdot z\\ \amp = x \cdot y \cdot z = 1 \cdot z = z\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.2. Sign in an ordered field.

    Let \(\of{ F , + , \cdot , \leq } \) be an ordered field. As in the case of the rational numbers \(\Q \) and the real numbers \(\R \text{,}\) we can define the sign of an element of \(F \) by comparison with the additive identity element \(0 \text{:}\)
    • Positivity.
      A non-zero element \(x \in F \setminus \set{ 0 } \) is called positive if \(0 \leq x \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    • Negativity.
      A non-zero element \(x \in F \setminus \set{ 0 } \) is called negative if \(x \leq 0 \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    • Zero.
      The additive identity element \(0 \in F \) is not positive or negative.
      🔗
      🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.2.a

    Let \(x \in F \text{.}\) Show that if \(x \neq 0 \text{,}\) then \(x \) and its additive inverse \(-x \) have opposite sign.
    🔗
    Solution.
    We first note that if \(-x = 0 \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} x = x + 0 = x + \of{ -x } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Thus if \(x \neq 0 \text{,}\) then \(-x \neq 0 \text{.}\) If \(x \) is positive, then \(0 \leq x \text{,}\) and so
