    \begin{equation*} -x = 0 + \of{ -x } \leq x + \of{ -x } = 0\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(-x \) is negative. On the other hand, if \(x \) is negative, then \(x \leq 0 \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} 0 = x + \of{ -x } \leq 0 + \of{ -x } = -x\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(-x \) is positive.
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.2.b

    More generally, show that for all elements \(x , y \in F \text{,}\) if \(x \leq y \) then \(-y \leq -x \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    We note that
    \begin{align*} -y \amp = 0 + \of{ -y } = x + \of{ -x } + \of{ -y }\\ \amp \leq y + \of{ -x } + \of{ -y } = y + \of{ -y } + \of{ -x } = 0 + \of{ -x } = -x\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.2.c

    Show that \(\of{ -1 } \cdot x = -x \) for all elements \(x \in F \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    We observe that
    \begin{equation*} x + \of{ \of{ -1 } \cdot x } = \of{ 1 \cdot x } + \of{ \of{ -1 } \cdot x } = \of{ 1 + \of{ -1 } } \cdot x = 0 \cdot x = 0\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(\of{ -1 } \cdot x \) is an additive inverse for \(x \text{.}\) Thus (c) in Problem 2.3.4.1 Uniqueness in a field implies that \(-1 \cdot x = -x \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.2.d

    Show that the product of two negative elements is positive.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x , y \in F \) be negative elements. Then \(x \neq 0 \) and \(y \neq 0 \text{;}\) we first argue that \(x \cdot y \neq 0 \text{.}\) Indeed, if \(x \cdot y = 0 \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} 1 \amp = 1 \cdot 1 = x \cdot x^{ -1 } \cdot y \cdot y^{ -1 } = x \cdot x^{ -1 } \cdot y \cdot y^{ -1 }\\ \amp = x \cdot y \cdot x^{ -1 } \cdot y^{ -1 } = 0 \cdot x^{ -1 } \cdot y^{ -1 } = 0\text{.} \end{align*}
    This contradiction implies that \(x \cdot y \neq 0 \text{.}\) We now observe that (a) implies that \(-x \) and \(-y \) are positive. Moreover, (c) implies that
    \begin{align*} x \cdot y \amp = 1 \cdot x \cdot y = \of{ -1 } \cdot \of{ -1 } \cdot x \cdot y\\ \amp = \of{ -1 } \cdot x \cdot \of{ -1 } \cdot y = \of{ -x } \cdot \of{ -y } \geq 0 \end{align*}
    is positive.
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.3. Infima and Dedekind completeness.

    Let \(\of{ F , + , \cdot , \leq } \) be an ordered field, and consider the map \(n \colon F \to F \) defined by the formula \(n \of{ x } = -x \text{;}\) that is, \(n \) sends an input \(x \in F \) to its additive inverse \(-x \text{.}\)
    🔗

    2.3.4.3.a

    Show that for a subset \(U \subseteq F \text{,}\) \(U \) is bounded from above if and only if \(n \of{ U } \) is bounded from below, and \(U \) is bounded from below if and only if \(n \of{ U } \) is bounded from above.
    🔗
    Solution.
    If \(U \) is bounded from above, then there is some element \(x \in F \) so that \(u \leq x \) for all \(u \in U \text{.}\) We observe that (b) in Problem 2.3.4.2 Sign in an ordered field implies that
    \begin{equation*} n \of{ x } = -x \leq -u = n \of{ u } \end{equation*}
    for all \(u \in U \text{,}\) and so \(n \of{ x } \) is a lower bound for \(n \of{ U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Similarly, if \(U \) is bounded from below, then there is some element \(x \in F \) so that \(x \leq u \) for all \(u \in U \text{.}\) We observe that (b) in Problem 2.3.4.2 Sign in an ordered field implies that
    \begin{equation*} n \of{ u } = -u \leq -x = n \of{ x } \end{equation*}
    for all \(u \in U \text{,}\) and so \(n \of{ x } \) is an upper bound for \(n \of{ U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    The reverse implications follow from the observation that \(n \) is its own inverse and then applying the above arguments to \(n \of{ U } \text{:}\) if \(n \of{ U } \) is bounded from above (resp. below), then \(U = n \of{ n \of{ U } } \) is bounded from below (resp. above).
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.3.b

    Show that a subset \(U \subseteq F \) has a supremum if and only if \(n \of{ U } \) has an infimum, and in this case
    \begin{equation*} \inf \of{ n \of{ U } } = n \of{ \sup \of{ U } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Similarly, show that \(U \) has an infimum if and only if \(n \of{ U } \) has a supremum, and in this case
    \begin{equation*} \sup \of{ n \of{ U } } = n \of{ \inf \of{ U } } \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Solution.
    The argument in the above proof of (a) shows that \(n \) interchanges upper and lower bounds. Specifically, \(n \) sends the set of upper bounds (resp. lower bounds) of a set \(U \) to the set of lower bounds (resp. upper bounds) of \(n \of{ U } \text{.}\) Moreover, by (b) of Problem 2.3.4.2 Sign in an ordered field, if \(x \leq y \text{,}\) then \(n \of{ y } \leq n \of{ x } \text{.}\) As such, \(n \) sends a least upper bound (resp. greatest lower bound) for \(U \) to a greatest lower bound (resp. least upper bound) for \(n \of{ U } \text{.}\) In particular, if \(U \) has a supremum (resp. infimum), then \(n \of{ U } \) has an infimum (resp. supremum). The converse holds by applying the same result to \(n \of{ U } \) and observing that \(n \) is an involution; that is, that \(n \circ n = \id_{ F } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    2.3.4.3.c

    Show that the ordered field \(\of{ F , + , \cdot , \leq } \) is Dedekind complete if and only if every non-empty subset of \(F \) which is bounded from below has an infimum.
    🔗
    Solution.
    If \(F \) is Dedekind complete, then every non-empty subset of \(F \) which is bounded from above has a supremum. Let \(U \) be a non-empty subset of \(F \) which is bounded from below. By (a) above, \(n \of{ U } \) is a non-empty subset of \(F \) which is bounded from above and hence has a supremum. Now (b) implies that \(U \) has an infimum.
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that every non-empty subset of \(F \) which is bounded from below has an infimum, and let \(U \) be a non-empty subset of \(F \) which is bounded from above. By (a) above, \(n \of{ U } \) is a non-empty subset of \(F \) which is bounded from below and hence has a supremum. Now (b) implies that \(U \) has a supremum. Thus \(F \) is Dedekind complete.
    🔗
    🔗

    II Point-Set Topology
    3 Metric Spaces
    3.1 Introduction to Metric Geometry
    3.1.4 Exercises

    Compute the distance between the given points in the indicated metric space.
    🔗

    3.1.4.1.

    Let \(d \) be the Euclidean metric on the real coordinate plane \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.1.4.1.a

    Compute \(d \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \text{,}\) where \(\vect{ x } = \of{ 3 , 2 } \) and \(\vect{ y } = \of{ -1 , 4 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(d \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } = \sqrt{ 20 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} d \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \amp = \sqrt{ \of{ 3 - \of{ -1 } }^{ 2 } + \of{ 2 - 4 }^{ 2 } } = \sqrt{ 4^{ 2 } + \of{ -2 }^{ 2 } }\\ \amp = \sqrt{ 16 + 4 } = \sqrt{ 20 }\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.1.b

    Compute \(d \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \text{,}\) where \(\vect{ x } = \of{ 3 , 4 } \) and \(\vect{ y } = \of{ 0 , 0 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(d \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } = 5 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} d \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \amp = \sqrt{ \of{ 3 - 0 }^{ 2 } + \of{ 4 - 0 }^{ 2 } } = \sqrt{ 3^{ 2 } + 4^{ 2 } }\\ \amp = \sqrt{ 9 + 16 } = \sqrt{ 25 } = 5\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.2.

    Let \(d_{ 3 } \) be the \(3 \)-metric on the real coordinate plane \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.1.4.2.a

    Compute \(d_{ 3 } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \text{,}\) where \(\vect{ x } = \of{ 3 , 2 } \) and \(\vect{ y } = \of{ -1 , 4 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(d_{ 3 } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } = \sqrt[ 3 ]{ 72 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} d_{ 3 } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \amp = \sqrt[ 3 ]{ \abs{ 3 - \of{ -1 } }^{ 3 } + \abs{ 2 - 4 }^{ 3 } } = \sqrt[ 3 ]{ 4^{ 3 } + 2^{ 3 } }\\ \amp = \sqrt[ 3 ]{ 64 + 8 } = \sqrt[ 3 ]{ 72 }\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.3.

    Let \(d_{ \infty } \) be the \(\infty \)-metric on the real coordinate plane \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.1.4.3.a

    Compute \(d_{ \infty } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \text{,}\) where \(\vect{ x } = \of{ 3 , 2 } \) and \(\vect{ y } = \of{ -1 , 4 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(d_{ \infty } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } = 4 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} d_{ \infty } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \amp = \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ 3 - \of{ -1 } } , \abs{ 2 - 4 } } }\\ \amp = \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ 4 } , \abs{ -2 } } } = \max \of{ \set{ 4 , 2 } } = 4\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.3.b

    Compute \(d_{ \infty } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \text{,}\) where \(\vect{ x } = \of{ 3 , 4 } \) and \(\vect{ y } = \of{ 0 , 0 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(d_{ 3 } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } = 4 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} d_{ \infty } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \amp = \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ 3 - 0 } , \abs{ 4 - 0 } } } = \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ 3 } , \abs{ 4 } } }\\ \amp = max \of{ \set{ 3 , 4 } } = 4\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.4.

    Let \(\delta_{ \R^{ 2 } } \) be the discrete metric on the real coordinate plane \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.1.4.4.a

    Compute \(\delta_{ \R^{ 2 } } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \text{,}\) where \(\vect{ x } = \of{ 3 , 2 } \) and \(\vect{ y } = \of{ -1 , 4 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\delta_{ \R^{ 2 } } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } = 1 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(\vect{ x } \neq \vect{ y } \text{,}\) \(\delta_{ \R^{ 2 } } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } = 1 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.4.b

    Compute \(\delta_{ \R^{ 2 } } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } \text{,}\) where \(\vect{ x } = \of{ 3 , 4 } \) and \(\vect{ y } = \of{ 0 , 0 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\delta_{ \R^{ 2 } } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } = 1 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(\vect{ x } \neq \vect{ y } \text{,}\) \(\delta_{ \R^{ 2 } } \of{ \vect{ x } , \vect{ y } } = 1 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Determine whether or not the given function is a metric.
    🔗

    3.1.4.5.

    Let \(d \colon \R \times \R \to \R \) be the Euclidean metric on the real numbers \(\R \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } \end{equation*}
    for all real numbers \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in \R \text{.}\) Verify that \(d \) is a metric on \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    For all real numbers \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in \R \text{,}\) if \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } = \abs{ 0 } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Conversely, if \(x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } = \max \of{ \set{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } } - \min \of{ \set{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } } \gt 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Since \(d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 0 \) if and only if \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) \(d \) is non-degenerate.
    🔗
    Since
    \begin{equation*} d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } = \abs{ x_{ 2 } - x_{ 1 } } = d \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } \end{equation*}
    for all real numbers \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) \(d \) is symmetric.
    🔗
    Finally, consider three points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in \R \text{.}\) If \(x_{ 1 } \geq x_{ 2 } \geq x_{ 3 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 3 } } \amp = x_{ 1 } - x_{ 3 } = x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 } - x_{ 3 }\\ \amp = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } + \abs{ x_{ 2 } - x_{ 3 } } = d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + d \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    If \(x_{ 1 } \geq x_{ 2 } \geq x_{ 3 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 3 } } \amp = x_{ 1 } - x_{ 3 } = x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 } - x_{ 3 }\\ \amp = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } - \abs{ x_{ 2 } - x_{ 3 } } \leq d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + d \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    If \(x_{ 2 } \geq x_{ 1 } \geq x_{ 3 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 3 } } \amp = x_{ 1 } - x_{ 3 } = x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 } - x_{ 3 }\\ \amp = -\abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } + \abs{ x_{ 2 } - x_{ 3 } } \leq d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + d \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    If \(x_{ 2 } \geq x_{ 3 } \geq x_{ 1 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 3 } } \amp = x_{ 3 } - x_{ 1 } = x_{ 3 } - x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 } - x_{ 1 }\\ \amp = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } - \abs{ x_{ 2 } - x_{ 3 } } \leq d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + d \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    If \(x_{ 3 } \geq x_{ 1 } \geq x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 3 } } \amp = x_{ 3 } - x_{ 1 } = x_{ 3 } - x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 } - x_{ 1 }\\ \amp = -\abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } + \abs{ x_{ 2 } - x_{ 3 } } \leq d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + d \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    Finally, if \(x_{ 3 } \geq x_{ 2 } \geq x_{ 1 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 3 } } \amp = x_{ 3 } - x_{ 1 } = x_{ 3 } - x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 } - x_{ 1 }\\ \amp = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } + \abs{ x_{ 2 } - x_{ 3 } } = d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + d \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    In any case, since \(d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + d \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{,}\) \(d \) satisfies the triangle inequality.
    🔗
    Since \(d \) is non-degenerate, symmetric, and satisfies the triangle inequality, it is a metric on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.6.

    Let \(\delta_{ X } \colon X \times X \to \R \) be the discrete metric on a set \(X \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \begin{cases} 0 \amp x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \\ 1 \amp x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 2 } \end{cases} \end{equation*}
    for all points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{.}\) Verify that \(\delta_{ X } \) is a metric on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    For all points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) if \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Conversely, if \(x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 1 \neq 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Since \(\delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 0 \) if and only if \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) \(\delta_{ X } \) is non-degenerate.
    🔗
    Consider two points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{.}\) If \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then \(x_{ 2 } = x_{ 1 } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 0 = \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Conversely, if \(x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then \(x_{ 2 } \neq x_{ 1 } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 1 = \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    In either case, since \(\delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } \text{,}\) \(\delta_{ X } \) is symmetric.
    🔗
    Finally, consider three points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \text{.}\) If \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 3 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } = 0 \leq \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{,} \end{equation*}
    since \(\delta_{ X } \) takes only non-negative values. On the other hand, suppose that \(x_{ 1 } \neq x_{ 3 } \text{.}\) Then it is not the case that \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \) and \(x_{ 2 } = x_{ 3 } \text{,}\) and so at least one of \(\delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \) and \(\delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \) is \(1 \text{.}\) We conclude that
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } = 1 \leq \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{.} \end{equation*}
    In either case, since \(\delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{,}\) \(\delta_{ X } \) satisfies the triangle inequality.
    🔗
    Since \(\delta_{ X } \) is non-degenerate, symmetric, and satisfies the triangle inequality, it is a metric on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.7.

    Let \(\rho \colon \R \times \R \to \R \) be the real-valued function defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not \(\rho \) is a metric on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    We note that
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ 0 , 1 } = 0 - 1 = -1 \neq 1 = 1 - 0 = \rho \of{ 1 , 0 }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(\rho \) is not symmetric. Since \(\rho \) is not symmetric, it is not a metric on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.8. Subspaces of discrete metric spaces.

    Let \(U \subseteq X \) be a subset of a discrete metric space \(\of{ X , \delta_{ X } } \text{.}\) Verify that the metric \(\rest{ \delta_{ X } }_{ U } \) induced on \(U \) by \(\delta_{ X } \) is the discrete metric \(\delta_{ U } \) on \(U \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\iota \colon U \to X \) be the inclusion map of \(U \) into \(X \text{,}\) and consider points \(u_{ 1 } , u_{ 2 } \in U \text{.}\) If \(u_{ 1 } = u_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then \(\iota \of{ u_{ 1 } } = \iota \of{ u_{ 2 } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \rest{ \delta_{ X } }_{ U } \of{ u_{ 1 } , u_{ 2 } } = \delta_{ X } \of{ \iota \of{ u_{ 1 } } , \iota \of{ u_{ 2 } } } = 0 = \delta_{ U } \of{ u_{ 1 } , u_{ 2 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    On the other hand, since \(\iota \) is injective, if \(u_{ 1 } \neq u_{ 2 } \text{,}\) then \(\iota \of{ u_{ 1 } } \neq \iota \of{ u_{ 2 } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \rest{ \delta_{ X } }_{ U } \of{ u_{ 1 } , u_{ 2 } } = \delta_{ X } \of{ \iota \of{ u_{ 1 } } , \iota \of{ u_{ 2 } } } = 1 = \delta_{ U } \of{ u_{ 1 } , u_{ 2 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    In either case, \(\rest{ \delta_{ X } }_{ U } \of{ u_{ 1 } , u_{ 2 } } = \delta_{ U } \of{ u_{ 1 } , u_{ 2 } } \text{,}\) and so \(\rest{ \delta_{ X } }_{ U } = \delta_{ U } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.4.9. Scalar multiples of metrics.

    Give an example of a real number \(\alpha \in \R \) and a metric \(\rho \colon X \times X \to \R \) so that \(\alpha \rho \) is not a metric.
    🔗
    Answer.
    Any non-positive real number \(\alpha \leq 0 \) and any metric \(\rho \colon X \times X \to \R \) on a set \(X \) with at least two distinct points are correct answers.
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since Lemma 3.1.4 Metrics are non-negative implies that metrics take only non-negative values, any non-positive real number \(\alpha \leq 0 \) and any metric \(\rho \colon X \times X \to \R \) on a set \(X \) with at least two distinct points are correct answers.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.5 Problems

    3.1.5.1. From pseudometrics to metrics.

    A real-valued function \(\rho \colon X^{ 2 } \to \R \) on the Cartesian square \(X^{ 2 } \) of a set \(X \text{,}\) which is called the ground set and whose elements are called points, is a pseudometric on \(X \) if it has the following properties:
    1. Identity.
      \(\rho \of{ x , x } = 0 \) for any point \(x \in X \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    2. Symmetry.
      \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } \) for any points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    3. Triangle inequality.
      \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \) for any points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    In this case, the ordered pair \(\of{ X , \rho } \) is called a pseudometric space.
    🔗

    3.1.5.1.a

    Let \(\rho \) be a pseudometric on a set \(X \text{,}\) and consider the binary relation \(\equiv_{ \rho } \) on \(X \) defined so that \(x_{ 1 } \equiv_{ \rho } x_{ 2 } \) if and only if \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 0 \text{.}\) Prove that \(\equiv_{ \rho } \) is an equivalence relation on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x \in X \text{,}\) and note that \(\rho \of{ x , x } = 0 \text{,}\) so that \(x \equiv_{ \rho } x \text{.}\) Since this holds for all points \(x \in X \text{,}\) \(\equiv_{ \rho } \) is reflexive.
    🔗
    Now let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(x_{ 1 } \equiv_{ \rho } x_{ 2 } \text{.}\) Then \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 0 \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 0 \text{;} \end{equation*}
    that is, \(x_{ 2 } \equiv_{ \rho } x_{ 1 } \text{.}\) Since this holds for all points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) \(\equiv_{ \rho } \) is symmetric.
    🔗
    Finally, let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(x_{ 1 } \equiv_{ \rho } x_{ 2 } \) and \(x_{ 2 } \equiv_{ \rho } x_{ 3 } \text{.}\) Then
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Lemma 3.1.4 Metrics are non-negative and the triangle inequality together imply that
    \begin{equation*} 0 \leq \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } = 0 + 0 = 0\text{,} \end{equation*}
    so that \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } = 0 \text{;}\) that is, \(x_{ 1 } \equiv_{ \rho } x_{ 3 } \text{.}\) Since this holds for all points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \text{,}\) \(\equiv_{ \rho } \) is transitive.
    🔗
    Since the binary relation \(\equiv_{ \rho } \) is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive, it is an equivalence relation on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.5.1.b

    Prove that a pseudometric \(\rho \) descends to a well-defined metric \(\overline{ \rho } \) on the quotient set \(X / { \equiv_{ \rho } } \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} \overline{ \rho } \of{ \class{ x_{ 1 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } , \class{ x_{ 2 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } , x_{ 4 } \in X \text{,}\) and suppose that \(x_{ 1 } \equiv_{ \rho } x_{ 3 } \) and \(x_{ 2 } \equiv_{ \rho } x_{ 4 } \text{.}\) Then
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 4 } } = 0\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so
    \begin{align*} \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \amp \leq \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 3 } , x_{ 4 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 4 } , x_{ 2 } }\\ \amp = \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 3 } , x_{ 4 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 4 } }\\ \amp = 0 + \rho \of{ x_{ 3 } , x_{ 4 } } + 0 = \rho \of{ x_{ 3 } , x_{ 4 } }\text{,} \end{align*}
    and similarly
    \begin{align*} \rho \of{ x_{ 3 } , x_{ 4 } } \amp \leq \rho \of{ x_{ 3 } , x_{ 1 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 4 } }\\ \amp = \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 4 } }\\ \amp = 0 + \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + 0 = \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    We conclude that \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 3 } , x_{ 4 } } \text{,}\) and so \(\overline{ \rho } \) is well-defined. It remains to show that \(\overline{ \rho } \) is a metric on \(X / { \equiv_{ \rho } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    To that end, first let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) and note that
    \begin{equation*} \overline{ \rho } \of{ \class{ x_{ 1 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } , \class{ x_{ 2 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } } = 0 \end{equation*}
    if and only if \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 0 \text{,}\) which occurs precisely when \(x_{ 1 } \equiv_{ \rho } x_{ 2 } \text{;}\) that is, \(\overline{ \rho } \of{ \class{ x_{ 1 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } , \class{ x_{ 2 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } } = 0 \) if and only if \(\class{ x_{ 1 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } = \class{ x_{ 2 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } \text{,}\) and so \(\overline{ \rho } \) is non-degenerate.
    🔗
    Now let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) and note that
    \begin{align*} \overline{ \rho } \of{ \class{ x_{ 1 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } , \class{ x_{ 2 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } } \amp = \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } }\\ \amp = \overline{ \rho } \of{ \class{ x_{ 2 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } , \class{ x_{ 1 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } }\text{,} \end{align*}
    so that \(\overline{ \rho } \) is symmetric.
    🔗
    Finally, let \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \text{,}\) and note that
    \begin{align*} \overline{ \rho } \of{ \class{ x_{ 1 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } , \class{ x_{ 3 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } } \amp = \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } }\\ \amp = \overline{ \rho } \of{ \class{ x_{ 1 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } , \class{ x_{ 2 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } } + \overline{ \rho } \of{ \class{ x_{ 2 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } , \class{ x_{ 3 } }_{ \equiv_{ \rho } } }\text{,} \end{align*}
    so that \(\overline{ \rho } \) satisfies the triangle inequality.
    🔗
    Since \(\overline{ \rho } \) is non-degenerate, symmetric, and satisfies the triangle inequality, it is a metric on \(X / { \equiv_{ \rho } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.5.2. Norm equivalence.

    Two norms \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 1 } , \norm{ \oparg }_{ 2 } \colon V \to \R \) on a real or complex vector space \(V \) are called equivalent if there are positive real numbers \(A , B \in \opint{ 0 }{ \infty } \) so that
    \begin{equation*} A \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 1 } \leq B \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \end{equation*}
    for all vectors \(\vect{ v } \in V \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.1.5.2.a

    Prove that norm equivalence is an equivalence relation on the set of norms on \(V \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    First let \(\norm{ \oparg } \colon V \to \R \) be a norm on \(V \text{,}\) and note that
    \begin{equation*} 1 \norm{ \vect{ v } } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v } } \leq 1 \norm{ \vect{ v } } \end{equation*}
    for all vectors \(\vect{ v } \in V \text{.}\) Thus \(\norm{ \oparg } \) and \(\norm{ \oparg } \) are equivalent, and so norm equivalence is reflexive.
    🔗
    Now let \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 1 } , \norm{ \oparg }_{ 2 } \colon V \to \R \) be norms on \(V \text{,}\) and suppose that \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 1 } \) and \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 2 } \) are equivalent. Then there are positive real numbers \(A , B \in \opint{ 0 }{ \infty } \) so that
    \begin{equation*} A \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 1 } \leq B \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \end{equation*}
    for all vectors \(\vect{ v } \in V \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \frac{ 1 }{ B } \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 1 } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \leq \frac{ 1 }{ A } \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 1 } \end{equation*}
    for all vectors \(\vect{ v } \in V \text{.}\) Thus \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 2 } \) and \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 1 } \) are equivalent, and so norm equivalence is symmetric.
    🔗
    Finally, let \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 1 } , \norm{ \oparg }_{ 2 } , \norm{ \oparg }_{ 3 } \colon V \to \R \) be norms on \(V \text{,}\) and suppose that \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 1 } \) and \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 2 } \) are equivalent and that \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 2 } \) and \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 3 } \) are equivalent. Then there are positive real numbers \(A , B , C , D \in \opint{ 0 }{ \infty } \) so that
    \begin{equation*} A \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 1 } \leq B \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{equation*} C \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 3 } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \leq D \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 3 } \end{equation*}
    for all vectors \(\vect{ v } \in V \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{align*} A C \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 3 } \amp \leq A \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 1 }\\ \amp \leq B \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 2 } \leq B D \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ 3 } \end{align*}
    for all vectors \(\vect{ v } \in V \text{.}\) Thus \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 1 } \) and \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ 3 } \) are equivalent, and so norm equivalence is transitive.
    🔗
    Since norm equivalence is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive, it is an equivalence relation on the set of norms on \(V \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.5.2.b

    Prove that for a given natural number \(n \in \N \text{,}\) any two \(p \)-norms \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ p } \) on \(n \)-dimensional real coordinate space \(\R^{ n } \) for \(1 \leq p \leq \infty \) are all equivalent.
    🔗
    Solution.
    We will show that any \(p \)-norm on \(\R^{ n } \) with \(1 \leq p \lt \infty \) is equivalent to the \(\infty \)-norm; (a) above implies that this suffices. To that end, let \(\vect{ v } = \of{ v_{ k } }_{ k = 1 }^{ n } \in \R^{ n } \text{,}\) and first note that
    \begin{equation*} \abs{ v_{ j } } = \of{ \abs{ v_{ j } }^{ p } }^{ \frac{ 1 }{ p } } \leq \of{ \sum_{ k = 1 }^{ m }{ \abs{ v_{ k } }^{ p } } }^{ \frac{ 1 }{ p } } = \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ p } \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(1 \leq j \leq m \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ \infty } = \max_{ 1 \leq j \leq m }{ \abs{ v_{ j } } } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ p }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    On the other hand, we note that
    \begin{align*} \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ p } \amp = \of{ \sum_{ j = 1 }^{ m }{ \abs{ v_{ j } }^{ p } } }^{ \frac{ 1 }{ p } } \leq \of{ \sum_{ j = 1 }^{ m }{ \max_{ 1 \leq k \leq m }{ \abs{ v_{ k } }^{ p } } } }^{ \frac{ 1 }{ p } }\\ \amp = \of{ m \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ \infty }^{ p } }^{ \frac{ 1 }{ p } } = m^{ \frac{ 1 }{ p } } \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ \infty }\text{.} \end{align*}
    Since
    \begin{equation*} 1 \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ \infty } \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ p } \leq m^{ \frac{ 1 }{ p } } \norm{ \vect{ v } }_{ \infty } \end{equation*}
    for all vectors \(\vect{ v } \in V \text{,}\) \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ p } \) and \(\norm{ \oparg }_{ \infty } \) are equivalent.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.5.3. Ultrametrics.

    A real-valued function \(\rho \colon X^{ 2 } \to \R \) on the Cartesian square \(X^{ 2 } \) of a set \(X \) is called an ultrametric on \(X \text{,}\) which is called the ground set and whose elements are called points if it satisfies the following conditions:
    • Non-degeneracy.
      For any points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{,}\) \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = 0 \) if and only if \(x_{ 1 } = x_{ 2 } \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    • Symmetry.
      \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } \) for any points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    • Strong triangle inequality.
      \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq \max \of{ \set{ \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } , \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } } } \) for any points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    If a real-valued function \(\rho \colon X^{ 2 } \to \R \) is an ultrametric on \(X \text{,}\) then the ordered pair \(\of{ X , \rho } \) is called an ultrametric space.
    🔗

    3.1.5.3.a

    Prove that any ultrametric is a metric.
    🔗
    Solution.
    An ultrametric is non-degenerate and symmetric, and so it remains only to show that it satisfies the triangle inequality. We first observe that given points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in X \) in an ultrametric space \(\of{ X , \rho } \text{,}\)
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } = \max \of{ \set{ \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } , \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } } } \geq \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 1 } } = 0\text{;} \end{equation*}
    that is, ultrametrics take only positive values. This implies that
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq \max \of{ \set{ \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } , \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } } } \leq \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } + \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \end{equation*}
    for all points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \text{,}\) so that \(\rho \) satisfies the triangle inequality and is therefore a metric on \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.5.3.b

    Fix a prime number \(p \in \N \text{.}\) The \(p \)-adic valuation \(\abs{ \oparg }_{ p } \colon \Q \to \Q \) is a rational-valued function defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} \abs{ q }_{ p } = \begin{cases} 0 \amp q = 0 \\ p^{ -k_{ p } \of{ q } } \amp q \neq 0 \text{,}\end{cases} \end{equation*}
    where \(k_{ p } \of{ q } \in \Z \) is the unique integer so that \(q = p^{ k_{ p } \of{ q } } \of{ \frac{ a }{ b } } \) for some integers \(a , b \in \Z \) which are not divisible by \(p \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Prove that the \(p \)-adic valuation \(\abs{ \oparg }_{ p } \) induces an ultrametric \(\rho_{ p } \colon \Q^{ 2 } \to \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} \rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } } = \abs{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } }_{ p }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    This ultrametric is called the \(p \)-adic metric on \(\Q \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    First let \(q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } \in \Q \) be rational numbers, and suppose that \(q_{ 1 } = q_{ 2 } \text{.}\) Then
    \begin{equation*} \rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } } = \abs{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } }_{ p } = \abs{ 0 }_{ p } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Conversely, now suppose that \(q_{ 1 } \neq q_{ 2 } \text{.}\) Then \(q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } \neq 0 \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } } = \abs{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } }_{ p } = p^{ -k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } } } \neq 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Since \(\rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } } = 0 \) if and only if \(q_{ 1 } = q_{ 2 } \text{,}\) \(\rho_{ p } \) is non-degenerate.
    🔗
    Now let \(q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } \in \Q \text{,}\) and observe that if
    \begin{equation*} q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } = p^{ k } \of{ \frac{ a }{ b } } \end{equation*}
    for some integers \(a , b \in \Z \) which are not divisible by \(p \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} q_{ 2 } - q_{ 1 } = p^{ k } \of{ \frac{ -a }{ b } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and \(p \) does not divide \(-a \) or \(b \text{.}\) Thus \(k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } } = k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 2 } - q_{ 1 } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{align*} \rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } } \amp = \abs{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } }_{ p } = p^{ -k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } } }\\ \amp = p^{ -k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 2 } - q_{ 1 } } } = \abs{ q_{ 2 } - q_{ 1 } }_{ p } = \rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 2 } , q_{ 1 } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    We conclude that \(\rho_{ p } \) is symmetric.
    🔗
    Finally, let \(q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } , q_{ 3 } \in \Q \text{,}\) and write
    \begin{align*} q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } \amp = p^{ j } \of{ \frac{ a }{ b } } \amp q_{ 2 } - q_{ 3 } \amp = p^{ k } \of{ \frac{ c }{ d } } \end{align*}
    for some integers \(j , k \in \Z \) and some integers \(a , b , c , d \in \Z \) which are not divisible by \(p \text{.}\) We observe that if \(j \leq k \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} q_{ 1 } - q_{ 3 } \amp = q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } + q_{ 2 } - q_{ 3 } = p^{ j } \of{ \frac{ a }{ b } } + p^{ k } \of{ \frac{ c }{ d } }\\ \amp = p^{ j } \of{ \frac{ a }{ b } + p^{ k - j } \of{ \frac{ c }{ d } } } = p^{ j } \of{ \frac{ a d + p^{ k - j } b c }{ b d } }\text{,} \end{align*}
    where \(p \) does not divide \(a d + p^{ k - j } b c \) or \(b d \text{.}\) On the other hand, if \(k \leq j \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} q_{ 1 } - q_{ 3 } \amp = q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } + q_{ 2 } - q_{ 3 } = p^{ j } \of{ \frac{ a }{ b } } + p^{ k } \of{ \frac{ c }{ d } }\\ \amp = p^{ k } \of{ p^{ j - k } \of{ \frac{ a }{ b } } + \frac{ c }{ d } } = p^{ j } \of{ \frac{ p^{ j - k } a d + b c }{ b d } }\text{,} \end{align*}
    where \(p \) does not divide \(p^{ j - k } a d + b c \) or \(b d \text{.}\)
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that
    \begin{equation*} k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 3 } } = \min \of{ \set{ k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } } , k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 2 } - q_{ 3 } } } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    so that
    \begin{align*} \rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } , q_{ 3 } } \amp = \abs{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 3 } }_{ p } = p^{ -k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 3 } } } = p^{ -\min \of{ \set{ k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } } , k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 2 } - q_{ 3 } } } } }\\ \amp = p^{ \max \of{ \set{ -k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } } , -k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 2 } - q_{ 3 } } } } } = \max \of{ \set{ p^{ -k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } } } , p^{ -k_{ p } \of{ q_{ 2 } - q_{ 3 } } } } }\\ \amp = \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } }_{ p } , \abs{ q_{ 2 } - q_{ 3 } }_{ p } } } = \max \of{ \set{ \rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } } , \rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 2 } , q_{ 3 } } } }\text{;} \end{align*}
    that is, \(\rho_{ p } \) satisfies the strong triangle inequality.
    🔗
    Since the \(p \)-adic metric \(\rho_{ p } \colon \Q \times \Q \to \R \) is non-degenerate, symmetric, and satisfies the strong triangle inequality, it is an ultrametric on \(\Q \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.1.5.3.c

    Prove that for all points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } \in X \) in an ultrametric space \(\of{ X , \rho } \text{,}\) at least two of the distances \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \text{,}\) \(\rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{,}\) and \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \) are equal.
    🔗
    Solution.
    This follows from applications of the strong triangle inequality. In particular, suppose that \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \neq \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{.}\) Up to renaming \(x_{ 1 } \text{,}\) \(x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) and \(x_{ 3 } \text{,}\) we may suppose that \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \lt \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{.}\) Then
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq \max \of{ \set{ \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } , \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Both \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \lt \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \) and \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{equation*} \max \of{ \set{ \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } , \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } } } \leq \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    On the other hand,
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \leq \max \of{ \set{ \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 1 } } , \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } } } = \max \of{ \set{ \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } , \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(\rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } = \max \of{ \set{ \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } , \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } } } \text{.}\) But we assumed that \(\rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \neq \rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{,}\) and so \(\rho \of{ x_{ 2 } , x_{ 3 } } = \rho \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 3 } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    The geometry of non-Archimedean metric spaces differs in several ways from our intuitions about Euclidean geometry informed by our experience of distance in the physical world. We will see several of these differences over the course of this chapter.
    🔗

    3.1.5.4. Reverse triangle inequality for norms.

    Prove that
    \begin{equation*} \abs{ \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } - \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } \end{equation*}
    for all vectors \(\vect{ v }_{ 1 } , \vect{ v }_{ 2 } \in V \) in a normed vector space \(\of{ V , \norm{ \oparg } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    The absolute homogeneity and subadditivity of the norm \(\norm{ \oparg } \) on \(V \) implies that
    \begin{align*} \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } \amp \leq \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } - \vect{ v }_{ 2 } + \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } }\\ \amp = \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } - \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } + \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } = \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } - \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } \end{align*}
    and
    \begin{align*} \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } \amp \leq \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } - \vect{ v }_{ 1 } + \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } }\\ \amp = \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } - \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } + \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } = \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } - \vect{ v }_{ 1 } }\\ \amp = \abs{ -1 } \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } - \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } = \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } - \vect{ v }_{ 2 } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    Thus
    \begin{equation*} \abs{ \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } } = \max \of{ \set{ \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } , \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 2 } } - \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } } } } \leq \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ 1 } - \vect{ v }_{ 2 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2 Convergence and Limits
    3.2.4 Exercises

    Describe in your own words the given mode of convergence. Do not just copy the definition.
    🔗

    3.2.4.1.

    Describe in your own words what it means for a sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of points \(x_{ n } \in X \) in a metric space \(\of{ X , \rho } \) to converge to a limit \(x \in X \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.2.4.2.

    Describe in your own words what it means for a sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of maps \(f \colon X \to Y \) from a set \(X \) to a metric space \(\of{ Y , \rho } \) to converge pointwise to a limit \(f \colon X \to Y \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.2.4.3.

    Describe in your own words what it means for a sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of maps \(f \colon X \to Y \) from a set \(X \) to a metric space \(\of{ Y , \rho } \) to converge uniformly to a limit \(f \colon X \to Y \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.2.4.4.

    Describe in your own words what it means for a map \(f \colon X \to Y \) from a metric space \(\of{ X , \rho_{ X } } \) to a metric space \(\of{ Y , \rho_{ Y } } \) to converge at a point \(c \in X \) to a limit \(l \in Y \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Determine whether the following sequences of real numbers converge or diverge in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If the given sequence converges, find the limit.
    🔗

    3.2.4.5.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(a_{ n } \in \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} a_{ n } = \frac{ \cos \of{ n \pi } }{ n }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges, find the limit, and express your answer in limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ a_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ \cos \of{ n \pi } }{ n } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that \(n_{ \epsilon } \gt \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } \) for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ a_{ n } , 0 } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ \cos \of{ n \pi } }{ n } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ \of{ -1 }^{ n } }{ n } }\\ \amp = \frac{ 1 }{ n } \leq \frac{ 1 }{ n_{ \epsilon } } \lt \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ a_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ \cos \of{ n \pi } }{ n } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.6.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(b_{ n } \in \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} b_{ n } = n \cos \of{ n \pi }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges, find the limit, and express your answer in limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges.
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(l \in \R \) be a real number. Then
    \begin{align*} d \of{ b_{ n } , l } \amp = \abs{ n \cos \of{ n \pi } - l } = \abs{ \of{ -1 }^{ n } n - l }\\ \amp = \abs{ n - l } = n - l \geq 1 \end{align*}
    for all even indices \(n \geq \ceil{ l } + 1 \text{,}\) and so \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) does not converge to \(l \text{.}\) Since \(l \) was chosen to be an arbitrary real number, the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.7.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ c_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(c_{ n } \in \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} c_{ n } = n \sin \of{ n \pi }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ c_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges, find the limit, and express your answer in limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ c_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ c_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ n \sin \of{ n \pi } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number, and observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ c_{ n } , 0 } = \abs{ n \sin \of{ n \pi } - 0 } = \abs{ 0 - 0 }\\ \amp = \abs{ 0 } = 0 \lt \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \in \N \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ c_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ c_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ n \sin \of{ n \pi } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.8.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(d_{ n } \in \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} d_{ n } = \frac{ 2 }{ n + 1 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges, find the limit, and express your answer in limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ d_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 2 }{ n + 1 } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \gt \frac{ 2 - \epsilon }{ \epsilon } \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ d_{ n } , 0 } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ 2 }{ n + 1 } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ 2 }{ n + 1 } } = \frac{ 2 }{ n + 1 }\\ \amp \leq \frac{ 2 }{ n_{ \epsilon } + 1 } \leq \frac{ 2 }{ \of{ \frac{ 2 - \epsilon }{ \epsilon } } + 1 } = \frac{ 2 }{ \of{ \frac{ 2 }{ \epsilon } } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ d_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 2 }{ n + 1 } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.9.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ e_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(e_{ n } \in \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} e_{ n } = \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ e_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges, find the limit, and express your answer in limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ e_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(2 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ e_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } } = 2\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \gt \frac{ 2 - \epsilon }{ \epsilon } \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ e_{ n } , 2 } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } - 2 } = \abs{ -\frac{ 2 }{ n + 1 } } = \frac{ 2 }{ n + 1 }\\ \amp \leq \frac{ 2 }{ n_{ \epsilon } + 1 } \leq \frac{ 2 }{ \frac{ 2 - \epsilon }{ \epsilon } + 1 } = \frac{ 2 }{ \of{ \frac{ 2 }{ \epsilon } } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ e_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(2 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ e_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } } = 2\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.10.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(f_{ n } \in \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} f_{ n } = \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges, find the limit, and express your answer in limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(\sqrt{ 2 } \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ f_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } } } = \sqrt{ 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The argument above in (5) implies that there is a natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \) so that
    \begin{equation*} d \of{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } , 2 } \lt 2 \epsilon \sqrt{ 2 } \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{gather*} \abs{ \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } } + \sqrt{ 2 } } \geq \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } } + \sqrt{ 2 } \geq \sqrt{ 2 - 2 \epsilon \sqrt{ 2 } } + \sqrt{ 2 } \geq 2 \sqrt{ 2 } \end{gather*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{align*} d \of{ f_{ n } , \sqrt{ 2 } } \amp = \abs{ \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } } - \sqrt{ 2 } } = \frac{ \abs{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } - 2 } }{ \abs{ \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } } + \sqrt{ 2 } } }\\ \amp = \frac{ d \of{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } , 2 } }{ \abs{ \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } } + \sqrt{ 2 } } } \lt \frac{ 2 \epsilon \sqrt{ 2 } }{ 2 \sqrt{ 2 } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(\sqrt{ 2 } \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ f_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 n }{ n + 1 } } } = \sqrt{ 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.11.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ g_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(g_{ n } \in \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} g_{ n } = \frac{ 3^{ n } }{ n! }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ g_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges, find the limit, and express your answer in limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ g_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ g_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 3^{ n } }{ n! } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We observe that an inductive argument shows that
    \begin{equation*} \frac{ 3^{ n - 1 } }{ \of{ n - 1 }! } \lt 1 \end{equation*}
    whenever \(n \geq 8 \text{.}\) Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \geq \max \of{ \set{ 8 , \frac{ 3 }{ \epsilon } } } \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ g_{ n } , 0 } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ 3^{ n } }{ n! } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ 3^{ n } }{ n! } } = \frac{ 3^{ n } }{ n! }\\ \amp = \of{ \frac{ 3 }{ n } } \of{ \frac{ 3^{ n - 1 } }{ \of{ n - 1 }! } } \lt \frac{ 3 }{ n_{ \epsilon } } \lt \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ g_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ g_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 3^{ n } }{ n! } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Determine whether the following sequences of real-valued functions converge or diverge both pointwise and uniformly in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If the given sequence converges pointwise, find the pointwise limit. Express your answer using limit notation. If the given sequence converges uniformly, find the uniform limit.

    3.2.4.12.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) of real-valued functions \(a_{ n } \colon \R \to \R \) defined by the formulae
    \begin{equation*} a_{ n } \of{ x } = \of{ x - \frac{ 1 }{ n } }^{ 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗

    3.2.4.12.a

    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges pointwise in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges pointwise, find the pointwise limit. Express your answer using limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    Let \(a \colon \R \to \R \) be the function defined by the formula \(a \of{ x } = x^{ 2 } \text{.}\) Then the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges pointwise to \(a \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ a_{ n } } = a\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(a \colon \R \to \R \) be the function defined by the formula \(a \of{ x } = x^{ 2 } \text{.}\) Fix a point \(x \in \R \text{,}\) and let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. We observe that if \(n \gt \frac{ 4 \abs{ x } }{ \epsilon } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} \frac{ 2 \abs{ x } }{ n } \lt \frac{ 2 \abs{ x } }{ \of{ \frac{ 4 \abs{ x } }{ \epsilon } } } = \frac{ \epsilon }{ 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Similarly, we observe that if \(n \gt \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 }{ \epsilon } } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{equation*} \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } \lt \frac{ 1 }{ \of{ \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 }{ \epsilon } } }^{ 2 } } = \frac{ \epsilon }{ 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \gt \max \of{ \set{ \frac{ 4 \abs{ x } }{ \epsilon } , \sqrt{ \frac{ 2 }{ \epsilon } } } } \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ a_{ n } \of{ x } , a \of{ x } } \amp = \abs{ \of{ x - \frac{ 1 }{ n } }^{ 2 } - x^{ 2 } } = \abs{ x^{ 2 } - \of{ \frac{ 2 }{ n } } x + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } - x^{ 2 } }\\ \amp = \abs{ -\frac{ 2 }{ n } x + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } } \leq \frac{ 2 \abs{ x } }{ n } + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } \lt \frac{ \epsilon }{ 2 } + \frac{ \epsilon }{ 2 } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } \of{ x } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(a \of{ x } \text{.}\) Since this holds for all points \(x \in \R \text{,}\) the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges pointwise to \(a \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ a_{ n } } = a\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.12.b

    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges uniformly in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges uniformly, find the uniform limit.
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) does not converge uniformly.
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    The dependence of \(n_{ \epsilon } \) on \(x \) in the above solution for (a) is a good sign that the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) does not converge uniformly. Indeed, we observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ a_{ n } \of{ n } , a \of{ n } } \amp = \abs{ \of{ n - \frac{ 1 }{ n } }^{ 2 } - n^{ 2 } } = \abs{ n^{ 2 } - \of{ \frac{ 2 }{ n } } n + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } - n^{ 2 } }\\ \amp = \abs{ -2 + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } } \geq 2 - \frac{ 1 }{ n } \geq 1 \end{align*}
    for all positive integers \(n \geq 1 \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) does not converge uniformly to \(a \text{.}\) Since \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges pointwise to \(a \text{,}\) this implies that the sequence \(\of{ a_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) does not converge uniformly.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.13.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) of real-valued functions \(b_{ n } \colon \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \to \R \) defined by the formulae
    \begin{equation*} b_{ n } \of{ x } = \frac{ x^{ 2 } }{ n }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗

    3.2.4.13.a

    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges pointwise in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges pointwise, find the pointwise limit. Express your answer using limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    Let \(b \colon \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \to \R \) be the constant function defined by the formula \(b \of{ x } = 0 \text{.}\) Then the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges pointwise to \(b \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ b_{ n } } = b\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(b \colon \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \to \R \) be the constant function defined by the formula \(b \of{ x } = 0 \text{.}\) Fix a point \(x \in \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \text{,}\) and let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \gt \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ \epsilon } \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ b_{ n } \of{ x } , b \of{ x } } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ x^{ 2 } }{ n } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ x^{ 2 } }{ n } }\\ \amp = \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ n } \leq \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ n_{ \epsilon } } \lt \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ \of{ \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ \epsilon } } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } \of{ x } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(b \of{ x } \text{.}\) Since this holds for all points \(x \in \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \text{,}\) the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges pointwise to \(b \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ b_{ n } } = b\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.13.b

    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges uniformly in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges uniformly, find the uniform limit.
    🔗
    Answer.
    Let \(b \colon \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \to \R \) be the constant function defined by the formula \(b \of{ x } = 0 \text{.}\) Then the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges uniformly to \(b \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \uniflim_{ n \to \infty }{ b_{ n } } = b\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    The dependence of \(n_{ \epsilon } \) on \(x \) in the above solution for (a) is a good sign that the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) does not converge uniformly. However, the bounds on the input \(-10 \leq x \leq 10 \) allow us to obtain uniform convergence.
    🔗
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \gt \frac{ 100 }{ \epsilon } \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ b_{ n } \of{ x } , b \of{ x } } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ x^{ 2 } }{ n } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ x^{ 2 } }{ n } }\\ \amp = \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ n } \leq \frac{ 10^{ 2 } }{ n_{ \epsilon } } \lt \frac{ 100 }{ \of{ \frac{ 100 }{ \epsilon } } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \) and points \(x \in \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ b_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges uniformly to \(b \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \uniflim_{ n \to \infty }{ b_{ n } } = b\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.14.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ c_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) of real-valued functions \(c_{ n } \colon \R \to \R \) defined by the formulae

    🔗
    Answer.

    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ c_{ n } } = c\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(c \colon \R \to \R \) be the constant function defined by the formula \(c \of{ x } = 0 \text{.}\) Fix a point \(x \in \R \text{,}\) and let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \gt \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ \epsilon } \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ c_{ n } \of{ x } , c \of{ x } } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ x^{ 2 } }{ n } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ x^{ 2 } }{ n } }\\ \amp = \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ n } \leq \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ n_{ \epsilon } } \lt \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ \of{ \frac{ \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ \epsilon } } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ c_{ n } \of{ x } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(c \of{ x } \text{.}\) Since this holds for all points \(x \in \R \text{,}\) the sequence \(\of{ c_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges pointwise to \(c \text{;}\) that is,

    Answer.

    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.

    \begin{align*} d \of{ c_{ n } \of{ \sqrt{ n } } , c \of{ \sqrt{ n } } } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ \of{ \sqrt{ n } }^{ 2 } }{ n } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ n }{ n } }\\ \amp = \abs{ 1 } = 1 \end{align*}
    for all positive integers \(n \geq 1 \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ c_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) does not converge uniformly to \(c \text{.}\) Since \(\of{ c_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges pointwise to \(c \text{,}\) this implies that the sequence \(\of{ c_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) does not converge uniformly.

    3.2.4.15.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real-valued functions \(d_{ n } \colon \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \to \R \) defined by the formulae
    \begin{equation*} d_{ n } \of{ x } = \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ x + n } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗

    3.2.4.15.a

    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges pointwise in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges pointwise, find the pointwise limit. Express your answer using limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    Let \(d \colon \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \to \R \) be the constant function defined by the formula \(d \of{ x } = 0 \text{.}\) Then the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges pointwise to \(d \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ d_{ n } } = d\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(d \colon \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \to \R \) be the constant function defined by the formula \(d \of{ x } = 0 \text{.}\) Fix a point \(x \in \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \text{,}\) and let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \gt \log_{ 3 } \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } } - x \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ d_{ n } \of{ x } , d \of{ x } } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ x + n } } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ x + n } } } = \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ x + n } }\\ \amp \leq \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ x + n_{ \epsilon } } } \lt \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ x + \log_{ 3 } \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } } - x } } = \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ \log_{ 3 } \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } } } }\\ \amp = \frac{ 1 }{ \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } \of{ x } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(d \of{ x } \text{.}\) Since this holds for all points \(x \in \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \text{,}\) the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges pointwise to \(d \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ d_{ n } } = d\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.15.b

    Determine whether the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges or diverges uniformly in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on the real numbers \(\R \text{.}\) If it converges uniformly, find the uniform limit.
    🔗
    Answer.
    Let \(d \colon \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \to \R \) be the constant function defined by the formula \(d \of{ x } = 0 \text{.}\) Then the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges uniformly to \(b \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \uniflim_{ n \to \infty }{ d_{ n } } = d\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    The dependence of \(n_{ \epsilon } \) on \(x \) in the above solution for (a) is a good sign that the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) does not converge uniformly. However, the bounds on the input \(-10 \leq x \leq 10 \) allow us to obtain uniform convergence.
    🔗
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \gt \ln \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } } + 10 \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ d_{ n } \of{ x } , d \of{ x } } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ x + n } } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ x + n } } } = \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ x + n } }\\ \amp \leq \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ -10 + n_{ \epsilon } } } \lt \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ -10 + \log_{ 3 } \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } } + 10 } } = \frac{ 1 }{ 3^{ \log_{ 3 } \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } } } }\\ \amp = \frac{ 1 }{ \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \) and points \(x \in \clint{ -10 }{ 10 } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ d_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges uniformly to \(d \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \uniflim_{ n \to \infty }{ d_{ n } } = d\text{.} \end{equation*}
