    Find all limit points and isolated points of the given subset of the Euclidean plane \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.2.4.16.

    Let \(U \) be the subset of the Euclidean plane \(\R^{ 2 } \) defined by
    \begin{equation*} U = \setmid{ \vect{ x } \in \R^{ 2 } }{ 1 \leq \norm{ \vect{ x } } \lt 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Find all limit points and isolated points of \(U \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    Every point \(\vect{ x } \in \R^{ 2 } \) so that \(1 \leq \norm{ \vect{ x } } \leq 2 \) is a limit point of \(U \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{,}\) and \(U \) has no isolated points.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.17.

    Let \(V \) be the subset of the Euclidean plane \(\R^{ 2 } \) defined by
    \begin{equation*} V = \setmid{ \vect{ x } \in \R^{ 2 } }{ \norm{ \vect{ x } } \in \N }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Find all limit points and isolated points of \(V \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    Every point \(\vect{ x } \in \R^{ 2 } \) so that \(\norm{ \vect{ x } } \in \N \setminus \set{ 0 } \) is a positive integer is a limit point of \(U \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{,}\) and \(\vect{ 0 } \) is the only isolated point of \(U \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.18.

    Let \(W \) be the subset of the Euclidean plane \(\R^{ 2 } \) defined by
    \begin{equation*} W = \setmid{ \of{ x , y } \in \R^{ 2 } }{ x , y \in \Z }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Find all limit points and isolated points of \(W \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(W \) has no limit points in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{,}\) and every point in \(W \) is an isolated point.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.19.

    Let \(X \) be the subset of the Euclidean plane \(\R^{ 2 } \) defined by
    \begin{equation*} X = \setmid{ \of{ x , y } \in \R^{ 2 } }{ x , y \in \Q }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Find all limit points and isolated points of \(X \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    Every point in \(\R^{ 2 } \) is a limit point of \(X \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{,}\) and \(X \) has no isolated points.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.20.

    Let \(Y \) be the subset of the Euclidean plane \(\R^{ 2 } \) defined by
    \begin{equation*} Y = \setmid{ \of{ x , y } \in \R^{ 2 } }{ x , y \notin \Q }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Find all limit points and isolated points of \(Y \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    Every point in \(\R^{ 2 } \) is a limit point of \(Y \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{,}\) and \(Y \) has no isolated points.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.21.

    Let \(Z \) be the subset of the Euclidean plane \(\R^{ 2 } \) defined by
    \begin{equation*} Z = \setmid{ \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ m } , \frac{ 1 }{ n } } \in \R^{ 2 } }{ m , n \in \N \setminus \set{ 0 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Find all limit points and isolated points of \(Z \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    The set of limit points of \(Z \) in \(\R^{ 2 } \) is
    \begin{equation*} Z' = \setmid{ \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ m } , 0 } \in \R^{ 2 } }{ m \in \N \setminus \set{ 0 } } \cup \setmid{ \of{ 0 , \frac{ 1 }{ n } } \in \R^{ 2 } }{ n \in \N \setminus \set{ 0 } }\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and every point of \(Z \) is an isolated point.
    🔗
    🔗
    Determine whether or not the given real-valued function converges at the given input. If the function converges, find the limit and express your answer using limit notation.
    🔗

    3.2.4.22.

    Consider the real-valued function \(f \colon \R \to \R \) defined piecewise by the formula
    \begin{equation*} f \of{ x } = \begin{cases} -1 \amp x \lt 0 \\ 0 \amp x = 0 \\ 1 \amp x \gt 0 \text{.}\end{cases} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not \(f \) converges at \(0 \text{.}\) If \(f \) converges at \(0 \text{,}\) find the limit and express your answer using limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(f \) does not converge at \(0 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We observe that
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ f \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ 1 } = 1 \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ f \of{ -\frac{ 1 }{ n } } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ -1 } = -1\text{.} \end{equation*}
    If \(f \) converged at \(0 \text{,}\) then the above limits to have the same value. Since they do not have the same value, \(f \) does not converge at \(0 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.23.

    Consider the real-valued function \(g \colon \R \to \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} g \of{ y } = y^{ 2 } - 2 y + 1\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not \(g \) converges at \(0 \text{.}\) If \(g \) converges at \(0 \text{,}\) find the limit and express your answer using limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(g \) converges at \(0 \) to \(1 \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ y \to 0 }{ g \of{ y } } = 1\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number, and let \(\delta = \min \of{ \set{ 1 , \frac{ \epsilon }{ 3 } } } \gt 0 \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ g \of{ y } , 1 } \amp = \abs{ y^{ 2 } - 2 y + 1 - 1 } = \abs{ y^{ 2 } - 2 y } = \abs{ y } \cdot \abs{ y - 2 }\\ \amp \lt \abs{ y - 0 } \cdot 3 = 3 d \of{ y , 0 } \lt 3 \delta \leq 3 \of{ \frac{ \epsilon }{ 3 } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all \(y \in \R \) so that \(0 \lt d \of{ y , 0 } \lt \delta \text{,}\) and so \(g \) converges to \(1 \) at \(0 \text{.}\) Since \(0 \) is a limit point of \(\R \text{,}\) this limit is unique; that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ y \to 0 }{ g \of{ y } } = 1\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.4.24.

    Consider the real-valued function \(h \colon \clopint{ 0 }{ \infty } \to \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} h \of{ z } = \sqrt{ z }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not \(h \) converges at \(4 \text{.}\) If \(h \) converges at \(4 \text{,}\) find the limit and express your answer using limit notation.
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\lim_{ x \to 4 }{ h \of{ z } } = 2 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number, and let \(\delta = \min \of{ \set{ 3 , 3 \epsilon } } \gt 0 \text{.}\) We observe that if \(d \of{ z , 4 } \lt 3 \text{,}\) then \(\abs{ z - 4 } \lt 3 \text{,}\) and so
    \begin{align*} z \amp \geq 1\\ \sqrt{ z } \amp \geq 1\\ \sqrt{ z } + 2 \amp \geq 3\\ \frac{ 1 }{ \sqrt{ z } + 2 } \amp \leq \frac{ 1 }{ 3 }\text{.} \end{align*}
    We conclude that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ h \of{ z } , 2 } \amp = \abs{ \sqrt{ z } - 2 } = \frac{ \abs{ z - 4 } }{ \sqrt{ z } + 2 }\\ \amp \lt \frac{ d \of{ z , 4 } }{ 3 } \lt \frac{ \delta }{ 3 } \leq \frac{ 3 \epsilon }{ 3 } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all \(z \in \R \) so that \(0 \lt d \of{ z , 4 } \lt \delta \text{,}\) and so \(h \) converges to \(2 \) at \(4 \text{.}\) Since \(4 \) is a limit point of \(\R \text{,}\) this limit is unique; that is,
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ z \to 4 }{ h \of{ z } } = 2\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.5 Problems

    3.2.5.1. Eventually constant sequences.

    A sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of elements \(x_{ n } \in X \) of a set \(X \) is called eventually constant if there is a point \(x \in X \) and a natural number \(n_{ 0 } \in \N \) so that \(x_{ n } = x \) for all indices \(n \geq n_{ 0 } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.2.5.1.a

    Prove that any eventually constant sequence of points in a metric space is convergent.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) be an eventually constant sequence of points \(x_{ n } \in X \) in a metric space \(\of{ X , \rho } \text{.}\) Then there is a point \(x \in X \) and a natural number \(n_{ 0 } \in \N \) so that \(x_{ n } = x \) for all indices \(n \geq n_{ 0 } \text{.}\) Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number, and observe that
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ x_{ n } , x } = \rho \of{ x , x } = 0 \lt \epsilon \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ 0 } \text{.}\) We conclude that the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(x \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.5.1.b

    Prove that any sequence of points in a discrete metric space is convergent if and only if it is eventually constant.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) be a sequence of points \(x_{ n } \in X \) in a discrete metric space \(\of{ X , \delta_{ X } } \text{.}\) If \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is eventually constant, then it is convergent by (a) above. Thus we may suppose that \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to some point \(x \in X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Let \(\epsilon = \frac{ 1 }{ 2 } \text{.}\) Since \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) is positive, there is a natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \) so that
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ n } , x } \lt \epsilon = \frac{ 1 }{ 2 } \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Since the values of \(\delta_{ X } \) are \(0 \) and \(1 \text{,}\) we conclude that
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ X } \of{ x_{ n } , x } = 0 \end{equation*}
    and hence \(x_{ n } = x \) for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is eventually constant.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.5.1.c

    Give an example of a sequence of real numbers which converges in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on \(\R \) but diverges in the discrete metric \(\delta_{ \R } \) on \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    \(\of{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \) in the Euclidean metric, but this sequence is not eventually constant and so does not converge in the discrete metric.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.5.2. Sequential convergence in the infinity metric.

    Let \(m \in \N \) be a natural number, and consider a sequence \(\of{ \vect{ x }_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of points \(\vect{ x }_{ n } = \of{ x_{ 1 , n } , \dotsc , x_{ m , n } } \in \R^{ m } \text{.}\) Prove that for any vector \(\vect{ x } = \of{ x_{ 1 } , \dotsc , x_{ m } } \in \R \text{,}\) the following are equivalent:
    1. The sequence \(\of{ \vect{ x }_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(\vect{ x } \) in the \(\infty \)-metric \(d_{ \infty } \) on \(\R^{ m } \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    2. For all indices \(1 \leq j \leq m \text{,}\) the sequence \(\of{ x_{ j , n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(x_{ j } \) in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on \(\R \text{.}\)
      🔗
      🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    First suppose that the sequence \(\of{ \vect{ x }_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(\vect{ x } \) in the \(\infty \)-metric \(d_{ \infty } \) on \(\R^{ m } \text{.}\) Fix an index \(1 \leq j \leq m \text{,}\) and let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. Then there is a natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \) so that
    \begin{equation*} d_{ \infty } \of{ \vect{ x }_{ n } , \vect{ x } } \lt \epsilon \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ x_{ j , n } , x_{ j } } \amp = \abs{ x_{ j , n } - x_{ j } } \leq \max_{ 1 \leq k \leq m }{ \abs{ x_{ k , n } - x_{ k } } }\\ \amp = \norm{ \vect{ x }_{ n } - \vect{ x } }_{ \infty } = d_{ \infty } \of{ \vect{ x }_{ n } , \vect{ x } } \lt \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{,}\) and so \(\of{ x_{ j , n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(x_{ j } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that for all indices \(1 \leq j \leq m \text{,}\) the sequence \(\of{ x_{ j , n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(x_{ j } \) in the Euclidean metric \(d \) on \(\R \text{.}\) Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. Then for each index \(1 \leq j \leq m \text{,}\) there is a natural number \(n_{ j , \epsilon } \in \N \) so that
    \begin{equation*} d \of{ x_{ j , n } , x_{ j } } \lt \epsilon \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ j , \epsilon } \text{.}\) Let \(n_{ \epsilon } = \max_{ 1 \leq j \leq m }{ n_{ j , \epsilon } } \in \N \text{,}\) and note that
    \begin{align*} d_{ \infty } \of{ \vect{ x }_{ n } , \vect{ x } } \amp = \norm{ \vect{ x }_{ n } - \vect{ x } }_{ \infty } = \max_{ 1 \leq j \leq m }{ \abs{ x_{ j , n } - x_{ j } } }\\ \amp = \max_{ 1 \leq j \leq m }{ d \of{ x_{ j , n } , x_{ j } } } \lt \max_{ 1 \leq j \leq m }{ \epsilon } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{,}\) and so the sequence \(\of{ \vect{ x }_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(\vect{ x } \) in the \(\infty \)-metric \(d_{ \infty } \) on \(\R^{ m } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.5.3. Alternative characterization of uniform convergence.

    Let \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) be a sequence of maps \(f_{ n } \colon X \to Y \) from a non-empty set \(X \neq \emptyset \) to a metric space \(\of{ Y , \rho } \text{.}\) Prove that \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to a map \(f \colon X \to Y \) from \(X \) to \(Y \) if and only if for all positive real numbers \(\epsilon \gt 0 \text{,}\) there is a natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \) so that
    \begin{equation*} 0 \leq \sup_{ x \in X }{ \rho \of{ f_{ n } \of{ x } , f \of{ x } } } \lt \epsilon \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    We observe that for a given index \(n \in \N \text{,}\)
    \begin{equation*} 0 \leq \sup_{ x \in X }{ \rho \of{ f_{ n } \of{ x } , f \of{ x } } } \lt \epsilon \end{equation*}
    if and only if
    \begin{equation*} \rho \of{ f_{ n } \of{ x } , f \of{ x } } \lt \epsilon \end{equation*}
    for all inputs \(x \in X \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges uniformly to \(f \) if and only if there is a natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \) so that either (and therefore both) of the above conditions holds for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.2.5.4. Uniqueness of map limits.

    Let \(f \colon X \to Y \) be a map from a metric space \(\of{ X , \rho_{ X } } \) to a metric space \(\of{ Y , \rho_{ Y } } \) which has at least two distinct points. Prove the following partial converse to Corollary 3.2.24 Uniqueness of map limits: If \(f \) converges at a point \(c \in X \) to a unique point \(l \in Y \text{,}\) then \(c \) is a limit point of \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    By contraposition. Suppose that \(c \) is an isolated point of \(Y \text{.}\) Then there are no sequences \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of points \(x_{ n } \in X \setminus \set{ c } \) which converge to \(c \text{,}\) and so for each point \(y \in Y \text{,}\) Theorem 3.2.19 Sequential characterization of map convergence implies that \(f \) converges at \(x \) to \(y \text{.}\) Since \(Y \) has at least two distinct points, then \(f \) does not converge to a unique limit at \(c \text{.}\)
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that if \(c \) is an isolated point, then \(f \) does not converge to a unique limit at \(c \text{.}\) Equivalently, if \(f \) converges at \(c \) to a unique limit, then \(c \) is a limit point of \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3 Limits in the Euclidean Metric
    3.3.4 Exercises

    Compute the limits of the given sequences of real numbers.
    🔗

    3.3.4.1.

    Compute \(\lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 3 } } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 3 } } } = 0 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } + \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 3 } } } \amp = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } } + \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 2 } } } + \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ n^{ 3 } } }\\ \amp = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.4.2.

    Compute \(\lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 2 n + 1 }{ 3 n } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 2 n + 1 }{ 3 n } } = \frac{ 2 }{ 3 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 2 n + 1 }{ 3 n } } \amp = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 2 n }{ 3 n } } + \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ 3 n } }\\ \amp = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 2 }{ 3 } } + \frac{ 1 }{ 3 } \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } }\\ \amp = \frac{ 2 }{ 3 } + 0 = \frac{ 2 }{ 3 }\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.4.3.

    Compute \(\lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 - n^{ 2 } }{ n } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 - n^{ 2 } }{ n } } = -\infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 - n^{ 2 } }{ n } } \amp = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } - n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } } - \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ n }\\ \amp = 0 - \infty = -\infty\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.4.4. Limits and strict comparison.

    Give examples of convergent sequences \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) and \(\of{ y_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(x_{ n } , y_{ n } \in \R \) so that \(x_{ n } \lt y_{ n } \) for all indices \(n \in \N \) but
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ y_{ n } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Solution.
    For each natural number \(n \in \N \text{,}\) let \(x_{ n } = -2^{ -n } \) and \(y_{ n } = 2^{ -n } \text{.}\) Then \(x_{ n } \lt y_{ n } \) for all indices \(n \in \N \text{,}\) but
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } = 0 = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ y_{ n } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗
    Compute the upper and lower limits of the given sequences of real numbers.
    🔗

    3.3.4.5.

    Compute \(\limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ 2^{ n } } \) and \(\liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ 2^{ n } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ 2^{ n } } = \liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ 2^{ n } } = \infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.4.6.

    Compute \(\limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ 2^{ -n } } \) and \(\liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ 2^{ -n } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ 2^{ -n } } = \liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ 2^{ -n } } = 0 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.4.7.

    Compute \(\limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ -2 }^{ n } } \) and \(\liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ -2 }^{ n } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ -2 }^{ n } } = \infty \) and \(\liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ -2 }^{ n } } = -\infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.4.8.

    Compute \(\limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ -2 }^{ -n } } \) and \(\liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ -2 }^{ -n } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(\limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ -2 }^{ n } } = \liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ -2 }^{ n } } = 0 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.5 Problems

    3.3.5.1. Limits and scalar multiplication.

    Let \(\norm{ \oparg } \) be a norm on a real or complex vector space \(V \text{.}\) Prove that
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \alpha_{ n } \vect{ v }_{ n } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \alpha_{ n } } \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \vect{ v }_{ n } } \end{equation*}
    for any convergent sequences \(\of{ \alpha_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) and \(\of{ \vect{ v }_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of scalars \(\alpha_{ n } \) and vectors \(\vect{ v }_{ n } \in V \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\alpha = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \alpha_{ n } } \) and \(\vect{ v } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \vect{ v }_{ n } } \text{.}\) Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number.
    \begin{align*} 0 \leq \norm{ \alpha_{ n } \vect{ v }_{ n } - \alpha \vect{ v } } \amp = \norm{ \alpha_{ n } \vect{ v }_{ n } - \alpha \vect{ v }_{ n } + \alpha \vect{ v }_{ n } - \alpha \vect{ v } } \leq \norm{ \alpha_{ n } \vect{ v }_{ n } - \alpha \vect{ v }_{ n } } + \norm{ \alpha \vect{ v }_{ n } - \alpha \vect{ v } }\\ \amp = \norm{ \of{ \alpha_{ n } - \alpha } \vect{ v }_{ n } } + \norm{ \alpha \of{ \vect{ v }_{ n } - \vect{ v } } } = \abs{ \alpha_{ n } - \alpha } \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } } + \abs{ \alpha } \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } - \vect{ v } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    Proposition 3.2.6 Sequential convergence and Proposition 3.3.1 Limits and vector arithmetic together imply that
    \begin{align*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ \abs{ \alpha_{ n } - \alpha } \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } } + \abs{ \alpha } \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } - \vect{ v } } } } \amp = \of{ \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \abs{ \alpha_{ n } - \alpha } } } \of{ \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } } } } + \abs{ \alpha } \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } - \vect{ v } } } \\ \amp = 0 \cdot \norm{ \vect{ v } } + \abs{ \alpha } \cdot 0 = 0 + 0 = 0\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    In summary, we have shown that
    \begin{equation*} 0 \leq \norm{ \alpha_{ n } \vect{ v }_{ n } - \alpha \vect{ v } } \leq \abs{ \alpha_{ n } - \alpha } \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } } + \abs{ \alpha } \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } - \vect{ v } } \end{equation*}
    and that
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \of{ \abs{ \alpha_{ n } - \alpha } \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } } + \abs{ \alpha } \norm{ \vect{ v }_{ n } - \vect{ v } } } } = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ 0 } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Theorem 3.3.3 Squeeze theorem implies that
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \norm{ \alpha_{ n } \vect{ v }_{ n } - \alpha \vect{ v } } } = 0\text{,} \end{equation*}
    and so \(\lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \alpha_{ n } \vect{ v }_{ n } } = \alpha \vect{ v } \) by Proposition 3.2.6 Sequential convergence.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.5.2. Limits and division.

    Prove that
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } } = \frac{ 1 }{ \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } } \end{equation*}
    for every sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of non-zero real numbers \(x_{ n } \in \R \setminus \set{ 0 } \) which converges to a non-zero real number limit
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } \neq 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(x = \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } \text{.}\) Since \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(x \) and \(x \neq 0 \text{,}\) there is a natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \) so that
    \begin{equation*} \abs{ x_{ n } - x } \lt \min \of{ \set{ \frac{ \abs{ x } }{ 2 } , \frac{ \epsilon \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ 2 } } } \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) We observe that if \(\abs{ x_{ n } - x } \lt \frac{ \abs{ x } }{ 2 } \text{,}\) then
    \begin{align*} \frac{ \abs{ x } }{ 2 } \amp = \abs{ x } - \frac{ \abs{ x } }{ 2 } \leq \abs{ x - x_{ n } } + \abs{ x_{ n } } - \frac{ \abs{ x } }{ 2 } \\\\ \amp \lt \frac{ \abs{ x } }{ 2 } + \abs{ x_{ n } } - \frac{ \abs{ x } }{ 2 } = \abs{ x_{ n } }\text{.} \end{align*}
    In particular,
    \begin{align*} \abs{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } - \frac{ 1 }{ x } } \amp = \abs{ \frac{ x - x_{ n } }{ x_{ n } x } } = \frac{ \abs{ x_{ n } - x } }{ \abs{ x_{ n } } \cdot \abs{ x } }\\ \amp \leq \frac{ \of{ \frac{ \epsilon \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ 2 } } }{ \of{ \frac{ \abs{ x } }{ 2 } } \abs{ x } } = \frac{ 2 \epsilon \abs{ x }^{ 2 } }{ 2 \abs{ x }^{ 2 } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) Thus
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } } = \frac{ 1 }{ x } = \frac{ 1 }{ \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.5.3. Squeezing to infinity.

    Let \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) and \(\of{ y_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) be sequences of real numbers \(x_{ n } , y_{ n } \in \R \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.3.5.3.a

    Prove that if \(x_{ n } \leq y_{ n } \) for all indices \(n \in \N \) and \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(\infty \text{,}\) then \(\of{ y_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(\infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(B \in \R \) be a real number. Since \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(\infty \text{,}\) there is a natural number \(n_{ B } \in \N \) so that \(x_{ n } \gt B \) for all indices \(n \geq n_{ B } \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{equation*} y_{ n } \geq x_{ n } \gt B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ B } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ y_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(\infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.5.3.b

    Prove that if \(x_{ n } \leq y_{ n } \) for all indices \(n \in \N \) and \(\of{ y_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \text{,}\) then \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(B \in \R \) be a real number. Since \(\of{ y_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \text{,}\) there is a natural number \(n_{ B } \in \N \) so that \(y_{ n } \lt B \) for all indices \(n \geq n_{ B } \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } \leq y_{ n } \lt B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ B } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    We can interpret the above results as versions of the Squeeze theorem for divergence to infinity.
    🔗

    3.3.5.4. Reciprocals of infinite limits.

    Prove that a sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of negative real numbers \(x_{ n } \lt 0 \) diverges to \(-\infty \) if and only if
    \begin{equation*} \lim_{ n \to \infty }{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } } = 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Solution.
    First suppose that \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \text{,}\) and let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \text{.}\) Then \(-\frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } \in \R \) is a real number, and so there is a natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \) so that
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } \lt -\frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{.}\) In particular,
    \begin{equation*} d \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } , 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } - 0 } = \abs{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } } = -\frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } \lt \epsilon \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{,}\) and so \(\of{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(\of{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges to \(0 \text{,}\) and let \(B \in \R \) be a real number. If \(B \leq 0 \) is non-negative, then
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } \lt 0 \leq B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \in \N \text{.}\) On the other hand, if \(B \lt 0 \) is negative, then \(-\frac{ 1 }{ B } \gt 0 \) is a positive real number, and so there is a natural number \(n_{ B } \) so that
    \begin{equation*} d \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } , 0 } \lt -\frac{ 1 }{ B } \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ B } \text{.}\) In particular,
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } = -\abs{ x_{ n } } = -\frac{ 1 }{ \abs{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } - 0 } } = -\frac{ 1 }{ d \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ x_{ n } } , 0 } } \lt B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ B } \text{,}\) and so \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.5.5. Divergence and upper/lower limits.

    In this problem, you will complete the proof of Theorem 3.3.20 Convergence and upper and lower limits. Let \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) be a sequence of real numbers \(x_{ n } \in \R \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.3.5.5.a

    Prove that \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(\infty \) if and only if
    \begin{equation*} \liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } = \infty\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Solution.
    We note that for a natural number \(m \in \N \) and a real number \(B \in \R \text{,}\) \(x_{ n } \geq B \) for all indices \(n \geq m \) if and only if
    \begin{equation*} \inf_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } \geq B\text{.} \end{equation*}
    First suppose that the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(\infty \text{.}\) Then for all real numbers \(B \in \R \text{,}\) there is a natural number \(m_{ B } \in \N \) so that \(x_{ n } \gt B + 1 \) for all indices \(n \geq m_{ B } \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{equation*} \inf_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } \geq \inf_{ n \geq m_{ B } }{ x_{ n } } \geq B + 1 \gt B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(m \geq m_{ B } \text{,}\) and so the sequence \(\of{ \inf_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } }_{ m = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(\infty \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } = \lim_{ m \to \infty }{ \inf_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } } = \infty\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(\liminf_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } = \infty \text{,}\) and let \(B \in \R \) be a real number. Then there is a natural number \(n_{ B } \in \N \) so that
    \begin{equation*} \inf_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } \gt B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(m \geq n_{ B } \text{.}\) In particular,
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } \geq \inf_{ k \geq n_{ B } }{ x_{ k } } \gt B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ B } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(\infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.3.5.5.b

    Prove that \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \) if and only if
    \begin{equation*} \limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } = -\infty\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Solution.
    We note that for a natural number \(m \in \N \) and a real number \(B \in \R \text{,}\) \(x_{ n } \leq B \) for all indices \(n \geq m \) if and only if
    \begin{equation*} \sup_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } \leq B\text{.} \end{equation*}
    First suppose that the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \text{.}\) Then for all real numbers \(B \in \R \text{,}\) there is a natural number \(m_{ B } \in \N \) so that \(x_{ n } \lt B - 1 \) for all indices \(n \geq m_{ B } \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{equation*} \sup_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } \leq \sup_{ n \geq m_{ B } }{ x_{ n } } \leq B - 1 \lt B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(m \geq m_{ B } \text{,}\) and so the sequence \(\of{ \sup_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } }_{ m = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \text{;}\) that is,
    \begin{equation*} \limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } = \lim_{ m \to \infty }{ \sup_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } } = -\infty\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    Conversely, now suppose that \(\limsup_{ n \to \infty }{ x_{ n } } = -\infty \text{,}\) and let \(B \in \R \) be a real number. Then there is a natural number \(n_{ B } \in \N \) so that
    \begin{equation*} \sup_{ n \geq m }{ x_{ n } } \lt B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(m \geq n_{ B } \text{.}\) In particular,
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } \leq \sup_{ k \geq n_{ B } }{ x_{ k } } \lt B \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \geq n_{ B } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges to \(-\infty \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.4 Open and Closed Sets
    3.4.4 Exercises

    Determine whether or not the given set is a neighborhood of the given point.
    🔗

    3.4.4.1.

    Let
    \begin{equation*} A = \opint{ 0 }{ 2 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ 0 \lt x \lt 2 } \end{equation*}
    be the open interval between \(0 \) and \(2 \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(A \) is a neighborhood of the point \(1 \) in the metric space \(\of{ \R , d } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(A \) is a neighborhood of \(1 \) in \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(A \) contains the open ball
    \begin{align*} \mB_{ 1 } \of{ 1 } \amp = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ \abs{ x - 1 } \lt 1 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ 0 \lt x \lt 2 }\\ \amp = \opint{ 0 }{ 2 } = A \end{align*}
    of radius \(1 \) and center \(1 \text{,}\) \(A \) is a neighborhood of \(1 \) in \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.4.4.2.

    Let
    \begin{equation*} B = \clint{ 0 }{ 2 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ 0 \leq x \leq 2 } \end{equation*}
    be the closed interval between \(0 \) and \(2 \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(B \) is a neighborhood of the point \(1 \) in the metric space \(\of{ \R , d } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(B \) is a neighborhood of \(1 \) in \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(B \) contains the open ball
    \begin{align*} \mB_{ 1 } \of{ 1 } \amp = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ \abs{ x - 1 } \lt 1 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ 0 \lt x \lt 2 }\\ \amp = \opint{ 0 }{ 2 } \subsetneq \clint{ 0 }{ 2 } = B \end{align*}
    of radius \(1 \) and center \(1 \text{,}\) \(B \) is a neighborhood of \(1 \) in \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.4.4.3.

    Let
    \begin{equation*} C = \clopint{ 0 }{ 2 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ 0 \leq x \lt 2 } \end{equation*}
    be the half-closed, half-open interval between \(0 \) and \(2 \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(C \) is a neighborhood of the point \(0 \) in the metric space \(\of{ \R , d } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(C \) is not a neighborhood of \(0 \) in \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We note that for any positive real number \(r \gt 0 \text{,}\) the open ball
    \begin{equation*} \mB_{ r } \of{ 0 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ \abs{ x - 0 } \lt r } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ -r \lt x \lt r } = \opint{ -r }{ r } \end{equation*}
    contains negative numbers. Thus any neighborhood of \(0 \) in \(\R \) contains negative numbers. Since every element of \(C \) is non-negative, \(C \) is not a neighborhood of \(1 \) in \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.4.4.4.

    Let
    \begin{equation*} D = 2 \Z = \setmid{ 2 k \in \Z }{ k \in \Z } = \set{ \dotsc , -6 , -4 , -2 , 0 , 2 , 4 , 6 , \dotsc } \end{equation*}
    be the set of even integers. Determine whether or not \(D \) is a neighborhood of the point \(0 \) in the discrete metric space \(\of{ \R , \delta_{ \R } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(D \) is a neighborhood of \(0 \) in \(\of{ \R , \delta_{ \R } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We note that since the discrete metric \(\delta_{ \R } \) takes only the values \(0 \) and \(1 \text{,}\)
    \begin{equation*} \mB_{ \frac{ 1 }{ 2 } } \of{ 0 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ \delta_{ \R } \of{ x , 0 } \lt \frac{ 1 }{ 2 } } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ \delta_{ \R } \of{ x , 0 } = 0 } = \set{ 0 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Since \(0 \in D \text{,}\) \(\mB_{ \frac{ 1 }{ 2 } } \of{ 0 } \subseteq D \text{,}\) and so \(D \) is a neighborhood of \(0 \) in \(\of{ \R , \delta_{ \R } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.4.4.5.

    Let
    \begin{equation*} E = \clint{ 0 }{ 1 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ 0 \leq x \leq 1 } \end{equation*}
    be the closed interval between \(0 \) and \(1 \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(E \) is a neighborhood of the point \(0 \) in the metric subspace \(\of{ \clint{ 0 }{ 2 } , d } \) of the real line \(\of{ \R , d } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(E \) is a neighborhood of \(0 \) in \(\clint{ 0 }{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since \(E \) contains the open ball
    \begin{align*} \mB_{ 1 } \of{ 0 } \amp = \setmid{ x \in \clint{ 0 }{ 2 } }{ \abs{ x - 0 } \lt 1 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ 0 \leq x \leq 2 \textrm{ and } -1 \lt x \lt 1 }\\ \amp = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ 0 \leq x \lt 1 } = \clopint{ 0 }{ 1 } \subseteq \clint{ 0 }{ 1 } = E \end{align*}
    of radius \(1 \) and center \(1 \text{,}\) \(E \) is a neighborhood of \(1 \) in \(\clint{ 0 }{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Determine whether the given subset of a metric space is open, closed, both open and closed, or neither open nor closed.
    🔗

    3.4.4.6.

    Let
    \begin{equation*} A = \opint{ 0 }{ 2 } = \setmid{ x \in \R }{ 0 \lt x \lt 2 } \end{equation*}
    be the open interval between \(0 \) and \(2 \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(A \) is open or closed in the metric space \(\of{ \R , d } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(A \) is open in \(\R \) but not closed.

    Answer.

    be the set of even integers. Determine whether or not \(D \) is open or closed in the discrete metric space \(\of{ \R , \delta_{ \R } } \text{.}\)

    \(E \) is closed in \(\clint{ 0 }{ 2 } \) but not open.

    Compute the diameter of the given subsets of metric spaces.

    🔗
    Let

    3.4.4.21.

    Determine whether \(U \) is bounded or unbounded in the discrete metric space \(\of{ \opint{ 0 }{ \infty } , \delta_{ \opint{ 0 }{ \infty } } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(U \) is bounded in \(\of{ \opint{ 0 }{ \infty } , \delta_{ \opint{ 0 }{ \infty } } } \)
    🔗
    🔗
    Compute the distance between the given bounded maps in the uniform metric.
    🔗

    3.4.4.22.

    Let \(f , g \colon \R \to \R \) be the constant real-valued functions defined by the formulae \(f \of{ x } = 2 \) and \(g \of{ x } = \pi \text{.}\) Compute the uniform distance \(d_{ \infty } \of{ f , g } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(d_{ \infty } \of{ f , g } = \pi - 2 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} d_{ \infty } \of{ f , g } \amp = \sup_{ x \in \R }{ d \of{ f \of{ x } , g \of{ x } } } = \sup_{ x \in \R }{ \abs{ 2 - \pi } }\\ \amp = \sup_{ x \in \R }{ \of{ \pi - 2 } } = \pi - 2\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.4.4.23.

    Let \(f , g \colon \clint{ -1 }{ 1 } \to \R \) be the real-valued functions defined by the formulae \(f \of{ x } = 2 \) and \(g \of{ x } = x^{ 2 } \text{.}\) Compute the uniform distance \(d_{ \infty } \of{ f , g } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(d_{ \infty } \of{ f , g } = 2 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} d_{ \infty } \of{ f , g } \amp = \sup_{ -1 \leq x \leq 1 }{ d \of{ f \of{ x } , g \of{ x } } } = \sup_{ -1 \leq x \leq 1 }{ \abs{ 2 - x^{ 2 } } }\\ \amp = \sup_{ -1 \leq x \leq 1 }{ 2 - x^{ 2 } } = 2 - 0 = 2\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.4.4.24.

    Let \(f , g \colon \opint{ 0 }{ 2 } \to \R \) be the real-valued functions defined by the formulae \(f \of{ x } = 2 - x \) and \(g \of{ x } = 1 + 3 x \text{.}\) Compute the uniform distance \(d_{ \infty } \of{ f , g } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(d_{ \infty } \of{ f , g } = 2 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    We compute
    \begin{align*} d_{ \infty } \of{ f , g } \amp = \sup_{ 0 \lt x \lt 2 }{ d \of{ f \of{ x } , g \of{ x } } } = \sup_{ 0 \lt x \lt 2 }{ \abs{ 2 - x - \of{ 1 + 3 x } } }\\ \amp = \sup_{ 0 \lt x \lt 2 }{ \abs{ 1 - 4 x } } = 7\text{.} \end{align*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.4.5 Problems

    3.4.5.1. Open and closed sets in metric subspaces.

    Let \(U \subseteq X \) be a subset of a metric space \(\of{ X , \rho } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.4.5.1.a

    Prove that a subset \(V \subseteq U \) is open in the metric subspace \(\of{ U , \rho } \) if and only if \(V = U \cap W \) for some open set \(W \subseteq X \) in \(X \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.4.5.1.b

    Prove that if a subset \(V \subseteq U \) is open in \(X \text{,}\) then it is open in \(U \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.4.5.1.c

    Construct an explicit counterexample to the converse of (b) above.
    🔗

    3.4.5.2. Interior, closure, and boundary of complements.

    Let \(U \subseteq X \) be a subset of a metric space \(\of{ X , \rho } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.4.5.2.a

    Prove that \(\Int \of{ X \setminus U } = X \setminus \Cl \of{ U } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.4.5.2.b

    Prove that \(\Cl \of{ X \setminus U } = X \setminus \Int \of{ U } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.4.5.2.c

    Prove that \(\Bd \of{ X \setminus U } = \Bd \of{ U } = \Cl \of{ U } \cap \Cl \of{ X \setminus U } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.4.5.2.d

    Prove that \(\Bd \of{ U } = \Cl \of{ U } \setminus \Int \of{ U } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.4.5.3. Closure and limit points.

    Let \(U \subseteq X \) be a subset of a metric space \(\of{ X , \rho } \text{.}\) Prove that \(\Cl \of{ U } = U \cup U' \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.4.5.4. Open balls in ultrametric spaces.

    Prove that any point in an open ball in an ultrametric space is its center.
    🔗

    3.4.5.5. Diameter in the real line.

    Let \(\emptyset \subsetneq U \subseteq \R \) be a non-empty subset of the real line \(\of{ \R , d } \text{.}\) Prove that \(U \) has diameter
    \begin{equation*} \diam \of{ U } = \sup \of{ U } - \inf \of{ U } \end{equation*}
    🔗

    3.5 Notions of Metric Continuity
    3.5.4 Exercises

    Determine whether or not the given map between metric spaces is continuous at the given point.
    🔗

    3.5.4.1.

    Let \(f \colon \R \to \R \) be the linear function defined by the formula \(f \of{ x } = 3 x - 1 \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(f \) is continuous at \(2 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(f \) is continuous at \(2 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number, and let \(\delta = \frac{ \epsilon }{ 3 } \gt 0 \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ f \of{ x } , f \of{ 2 } } \amp = \abs{ \of{ 3 x - 1 } - \of{ 3 \of{ 2 } - 1 } }\\ \amp = \abs{ 3 x - 6 } = 3 \abs{ x - 2 } = 3 d \of{ x , 2 } \lt 3 \delta = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all points \(x \in \R \) so that \(d \of{ x , 2 } \lt \delta \text{.}\) Thus \(f \) is continuous at \(2 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.5.4.2.

    Let \(g \colon \R \to \R \) be the linear function defined by the formula \(f \of{ y } = 2 y^{ 2 } - 1 \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(g \) is continuous at \(4 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(g \) is continuous at \(4 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number, and let
    \begin{equation*} \delta = \max \of{ \set{ 1 , \frac{ \epsilon }{ 18 } } } \gt 0\text{.} \end{equation*}
    We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ g \of{ y } , g \of{ 4 } } \amp = \abs{ \of{ 2 y^{ 2 } - 1 } - \of{ 2 \of{ 4 }^{ 2 } - 1 } } = \abs{ 2 y^{ 2 } - 32 } = 2 \abs{ y + 4 } \cdot \abs{ y - 4 }\\ \amp = 2 \abs{ y + 4 } d \of{ y , 4 } \lt 2 \cdot 9 \delta \lt \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all points \(y \in \R \) so that \(d \of{ y , 4 } \lt \delta \text{.}\) Thus \(g \) is continuous at \(4 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.5.4.3.

    Let \(h \colon \clopint{ 0 }{ \infty } \to \R \) be the linear function defined by the formula \(h \of{ z } = \sqrt{ z } \text{.}\) Determine whether or not \(h \) is continuous at \(1 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(h \) is continuous at \(4 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number, and let \(\delta = \epsilon \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{equation*} d \of{ h \of{ z } , h \of{ 1 } } = \abs{ \sqrt{ z } - \sqrt{ 1 } } = \abs{ \frac{ z - 1 }{ \sqrt{ z } + 1 } } \lt \frac{ \delta }{ 1 } = \epsilon \end{equation*}
    for all points \(z \in \R \) so that \(d \of{ z , 1 } \lt \delta \text{.}\) Thus \(h \) is continuous at \(1 \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.5.4.4. Uniform and Lipschitz continuity.

    Let \(-\infty \lt a \lt b \lt \infty \) be real numbers, and consider the real-valued function \(f \colon \clint{ a }{ b } \to \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} f \of{ x } = x^{ 3 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗

    3.5.4.4.a

    Verify that \(f \) is not uniformly continuous.
    🔗
    Solution.
    We observe that for any positive real number \(\delta \gt 0 \text{,}\)
    \begin{align*} d \of{ f \of{ x + \frac{ \delta }{ 2 } } , f \of{ x } } \amp = \abs{ \of{ x + \frac{ \delta }{ 2 } }^{ 3 } - x^{ 3 } }\\ \amp = \abs{ \frac{ \delta }{ 2 } x^{ 2 } + \frac{ \delta^{ 2 } }{ 4 } x + \frac{ \delta^{ 3 } }{ 8 } } \end{align*}
    is the absolute value of a quadratic function of \(x \) and is therefore unbounded from above. This implies that for any positive real number \(\delta \gt 0 \text{,}\) there is some real number \(x \in \R \) so that \(d \of{ f \of{ x + \frac{ \delta }{ 2 } } , f \of{ x } } \geq 1 \text{,}\) and so \(f \) is not uniformly continuous.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.5.4.4.b

    Without appealing to Proposition 3.5.20 Lipschitz continuity implies uniform continuity, verify that \(f \) is not Lipschitz continuous.
    🔗
    Solution.
    We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ f \of{ x_{ 1 } } , f \of{ x_{ 2 } } } \amp = \abs{ x_{ 1 }^{ 3 } - x_{ 2 }^{ 3 } } \amp = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } \cdot \abs{ x_{ 1 }^{ 2 } + x_{ 1 } x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 }^{ 2 } }\\ \amp = \abs{ x_{ 1 }^{ 2 } + x_{ 1 } x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 }^{ 2 } } \cdot d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \end{align*}
    for any points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in \R \text{.}\) Since \(x_{ 1 }^{ 2 } + x_{ 1 } x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 }^{ 2 } \) is a quadratic polynomial in \(x_{ 1 } \) and \(x_{ 2 } \text{,}\) it is not bounded in absolute value, and so \(f \) cannot be Lipschitz continuous.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.5.4.4.c

    Verify that the restriction of \(f \) to a closed interval \(\clint{ a }{ b } \subsetneq \R \) is uniformly continuous.
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number, and consider the positive real number
    \begin{equation*} \delta = \frac{ \epsilon }{ 3 \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ a }^{ 2 } , \abs{ b }^{ 2 } } } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    We observe that
    \begin{align*} \abs{ x_{ 1 }^{ 3 } - x_{ 2 }^{ 3 } } \amp = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } \cdot \abs{ x_{ 1 }^{ 2 } + x_{ 1 } x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 }^{ 2 } }\\ \amp \leq d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \cdot 3 \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ a }^{ 2 } , \abs{ b }^{ 2 } } } \lt 3 \delta \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ a }^{ 2 } , \abs{ b }^{ 2 } } } = \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in \clint{ a }{ b } \) so that \(d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \lt \delta \text{.}\) Thus the restriction of \(f \) to \(\clint{ a }{ b } \) is uniformly continuous.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.5.4.4.d

    Verify that the restriction of \(f \) to a closed interval \(\clint{ a }{ b } \subsetneq \R \) is Lipschitz continuous.
    🔗
    Solution.
    We observe that
    \begin{align*} \abs{ x_{ 1 }^{ 3 } - x_{ 2 }^{ 3 } } \amp = \abs{ x_{ 1 } - x_{ 2 } } \cdot \abs{ x_{ 1 }^{ 2 } + x_{ 1 } x_{ 2 } + x_{ 2 }^{ 2 } }\\ \amp \leq d \of{ x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } } \cdot 3 \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ a }^{ 2 } , \abs{ b }^{ 2 } } } \end{align*}
    for all points \(x_{ 1 } , x_{ 2 } \in \clint{ a }{ b } \text{.}\) Thus the restriction of \(f \) to \(\clint{ a }{ b } \) is Lipschitz continuous, with Lipschitz constant
    \begin{equation*} L_{ f } \leq 3 \max \of{ \set{ \abs{ a }^{ 2 } , \abs{ b }^{ 2 } } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗
    🔗

    3.5.5 Problems

    3.5.5.1. Continuity of rational functions.

    3.5.5.1.a

    Prove using one or more characterizations of continuity that the real-valued function \(f \colon \R \to \R \) defined by the formula \(f \of{ x } = x \) is continuous.
    🔗

    3.5.5.1.b

    Argue that any polynomial function is continuous.
    🔗

    3.5.5.1.c

    Argue that any rational function is continuous on its implicit domain.
    🔗

    3.5.5.2. Uniform limits of uniformly continuous maps.

    Let \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) be a sequence of uniformly continuous maps \(f_{ n } \colon X \to Y \) from a metric space \(\of{ X , \rho_{ X } } \) to a metric space \(\of{ Y , \rho_{ Y } } \text{.}\) Prove that if \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges uniformly, the uniform limit is also uniformly continuous.
    🔗

    3.5.5.3. Continuous pointwise limits of discontinuous maps.

    We have seen in Theorem 3.5.9 Uniform limits of continuous maps and Problem 3.5.5.2 Uniform limits of uniformly continuous maps that uniform limits preserve continuity and uniform continuity. In this problem, we will construct counterexamples to the converses of these results.
    🔗

    3.5.5.3.a

    Give an example of a sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of continuous maps \(f_{ n } \colon X \to Y \) from a metric space \(X \) to a metric space \(Y \) which converges pointwise but not uniformly to a continuous map \(f \colon X \to Y \) from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.5.5.3.b

    Give an example of a sequence \(\of{ g_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of discontinuous maps \(g_{ n } \colon X \to Y \) from a metric space \(X \) to a metric space \(Y \) which converges uniformly to a continuous map \(g \colon X \to Y \) from \(X \) to \(Y \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.5.5.4. Uniform limits of Lipschitz continuous maps.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) of real-valued functions \(f_{ n } \colon \clopint{ 0 }{ \infty } \to \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} f_{ n } \of{ x } = \sqrt{ x + \frac{ 1 }{ n } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗

    3.5.5.4.a

    Show that \(f_{ n } \) is Lipschitz continuous for each index \(n \geq 1 \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.5.5.4.b

    Show that the sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges uniformly, but the uniform limit is not Lipschitz continuous.
    🔗

    3.5.5.4.c

    Prove that the limit of a uniformly convergent sequence \(\of{ g_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of Lipschitz continuous maps \(g_{ n } \colon X \to Y \) from a metric space \(\of{ X , \rho_{ X } } \) to a metric space \(\of{ Y , \rho_{ Y } } \) is Lipschitz continuous if the sequence \(\of{ L_{ g_{ n } } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of Lipschitz constants \(L_{ g_{ n } } \) is bounded from above.
    🔗

    3.6 Completeness and Compactness
    3.6.4 Exercises

    Determine whether or not the given sequence of points in a metric space is Cauchy.
    🔗

    3.6.4.1.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(x_{ n } \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } = 2 + 3^{ -n }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is a Cauchy sequence in the real line \(\of{ \R , d } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is a Cauchy sequence in the real line \(\of{ \R , d } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Let \(\epsilon \gt 0 \) be a positive real number. The Archimedean principle implies that
    \begin{equation*} n_{ \epsilon } \gt \log_{ 3 } \of{ \frac{ 1 }{ \epsilon } } \end{equation*}
    for some natural number \(n_{ \epsilon } \in \N \text{.}\) We observe that
    \begin{align*} d \of{ x_{ m } , x_{ n } } \amp = \abs{ \of{ 2 + 3^{ -m } } - \of{ 2 + 3^{ -n } } } = 3^{ -\min \of{ \set{ m , n } } } - 3^{ -\max \of{ \set{ m , n } } }\\ \amp = 3^{ -\min \of{ \set{ m , n } } } \of{ 1 - 3^{ \min \of{ \set{ m , n } } - \max \of{ \set{ m , n } } } } \leq 3^{ -n_{ \epsilon } } \lt \epsilon \end{align*}
    for all indices \(m , n \geq n_{ \epsilon } \text{,}\) and so the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is Cauchy.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.6.4.2.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of real numbers \(x_{ n } \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } = 2 + 3^{ -n }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is a Cauchy sequence in the discrete metric space \(\of{ \R , \delta_{ \R } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is not a Cauchy sequence in the discrete metric space \(\of{ \R , \delta_{ \R } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    Since
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } = 3^{ -n } \gt 3^{ -\of{ n + 1 } } = x_{ n + 1 } \end{equation*}
    for all indices \(n \in \N \text{,}\)
    \begin{equation*} \delta_{ \R } \of{ x_{ n } , x_{ n + 1 } } = 1 \end{equation*}
    for all such indices. In particular, there is no natural number \(n_{ 1 } \in \N \) so that \(\delta_{ \R } \of{ x_{ m } , x_{ n } } \lt 1 \) for all indices \(m , n \geq n_{ 1 } \text{.}\) Thus the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is not a Cauchy sequence in the discrete metric space \(\of{ \R , \delta_{ \R } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.6.4.3.

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of bounded real-valued functions \(f_{ n } \colon \clopint{ 0 }{ \infty } \to \R \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} f_{ n } \of{ x } = \frac{ 1 }{ x^{ n } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether or not the sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is a Cauchy sequence in the metric space \(\of{ \Bdd \of{ \clopint{ 1 }{ \infty } } , d_{ \infty } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    The sequence \(\of{ f_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is a Cauchy sequence in the metric space \(\of{ \Bdd \of{ \clopint{ 1 }{ \infty } } , d_{ \infty } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Determine whether the given subset of Euclidean space is complete or incomplete.
    🔗

    3.6.4.4.

    Let \(U \) be the subset of the Euclidean plane \(\R^{ 2 } \) defined by
    \begin{equation*} U = \setmid{ \vect{ x } \in \R^{ 2 } }{ \vect{ x } \neq \vect{ 0 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether \(U \) is complete or incomplete in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(U \) is incomplete in \(\R^{ 2 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    For any vector \(\vect{ u } \in U \text{,}\) the sequence \(\of{ \vect{ u }_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} \vect{ u }_{ n } = \frac{ \vect{ u } }{ n } \end{equation*}
    is a Cauchy sequence which does not converge to a limit in \(U \text{,}\) and so \(U \) is not complete.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.6.4.5.

    Let \(V \) be the subset of \(3 \)-dimensional Euclidean space \(\R^{ 3 } \) defined by
    \begin{equation*} V = \setmid{ \vect{ x } \in \R^{ 3 } }{ \norm{ \vect{ x } } = 2 }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Determine whether \(V \) is complete or incomplete in \(\R^{ 3 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    \(V \) is complete in \(\R^{ 3 } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    \(V \) is the pre-image of the closed set \(\set{ 2 } \) of \(\R \) under the continuous map \(\norm{ \oparg } \colon \R^{ 3 } \to \R \text{,}\) and so \(V \) is closed. Since \(V \) is a closed subset of a complete metric space, it is complete.
    🔗
    🔗

    3.6.4.6.

    Determine whether the irrational numbers \(\R \setminus \Q \) is complete or incomplete in the real line \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Answer.
    The irrational numbers \(\R \setminus \Q \) are incomplete in the real line \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗
    Solution.
    For any irrational number \(x \in \R \setminus \Q \text{,}\) the sequence \(\of{ x_{ n } }_{ n = 1 }^{ \infty } \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} x_{ n } = \frac{ x }{ n } \end{equation*}
    is a Cauchy sequence of irrational numbers which does not converge to a limit in \(\R \setminus \Q \text{,}\) and so the irrational numbers \(\R \setminus \Q \) are incomplete in the real line \(\R \text{.}\)
    🔗
    🔗

    3.6.5 Problems

    3.6.5.1. Completeness of discrete metric spaces.

    3.6.5.1.a

    Prove that any discrete metric space is complete.
    🔗

    3.6.5.1.b

    Prove that any metric space with finite ground set is complete.
    🔗

    3.6.5.1.c

    Consider the metric subspace \(\of{ X , d } \) of the real line \(\of{ \R , d } \) with ground set
    \begin{equation*} X = \setmid{ \frac{ 1 }{ n } \in \R }{ n \in \N \setminus \set{ 0 } }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Argue that \(\of{ X , d } \) is a counterexample to the false claim that any metric space whose points are all isolated points is complete.
    🔗

    3.6.5.2. Incompleteness of the \(p \)-adic metric.

    Fix a prime number \(p \in \N \text{,}\) and recall that the \(p \)-adic valuation \(\abs{ \oparg }_{ p } \colon \Q \to \Q \) is a rational-valued function defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} \abs{ q }_{ p } = \begin{cases} 0 \amp q = 0 \\ p^{ -k_{ p } \of{ q } } \amp q \neq 0 \text{,}\end{cases} \end{equation*}
    where \(k_{ p } \of{ q } \in \Z \) is the unique integer so that \(q = p^{ k_{ p } \of{ q } } \of{ \frac{ a }{ b } } \) for some integers \(a , b \in \Z \) which are not divisible by \(p \text{.}\)
    🔗
    Moreover, recall from (b) of Problem 3.1.5.3 Ultrametrics that the \(p \)-adic valuation \(\abs{ \oparg }_{ p } \) induces an ultrametric \(\rho_{ p } \colon \Q^{ 2 } \to \R \) called the \(p \)-adic metric defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} \rho_{ p } \of{ q_{ 1 } , q_{ 2 } } = \abs{ q_{ 1 } - q_{ 2 } }_{ p }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    🔗

    3.6.5.2.a

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ q_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of rational numbers \(q_{ n } \in \Q \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} q_{ n } = \sum_{ k = 0 }^{ n }{ p^{ k } } = 1 + p + p^{ 2 } + \dotsb + p^{ n }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Show that \(\of{ q_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is a Cauchy sequence in \(\of{ \Q , \rho_{ p } } \text{.}\)
    🔗

    3.6.5.2.b

    Consider the sequence \(\of{ r_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) of rational numbers \(r_{ n } \in \Q \) defined by the formula
    \begin{equation*} r_{ n } = \sum_{ k = 0 }^{ n }{ p^{ k! } } = p + p + p^{ 2 } + p^{ 6 } + \dotsb + p^{ n! }\text{.} \end{equation*}
    Show that \(\of{ r_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) is a Cauchy sequence in \(\of{ \Q , \rho_{ p } } \text{.}\)
    🔗
    While the Cauchy sequence \(\of{ q_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) converges in the \(p \)-adic metric to a rational number, the Cauchy sequence \(\of{ r_{ n } }_{ n = 0 }^{ \infty } \) diverges and therefore is a witness to the incompleteness of \(\of{ \Q , \rho_{ p } } \text{.}\)
